Galen · machine first draft

On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs

De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus · Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377 · books 1-11

No complete published translation into English by a scholar that we know of. An English translation of books 1-5 by J. Wilkins is announced in the Cambridge Galen Translations. Books 6-8: a French translation with the Greek by C. Manco (doctoral thesis, Montpellier and Bologna, 2020). The prefaces of books 6-11 and the first two entries of book 6: English by M. Coughlin (ancientmedicine.org, 2023). All eleven books: a free French version by H. Fayard (1548). An unreviewed machine translation into English of Kühn XI and XII, the volumes containing these books, was posted at Source Library (sourcelibrary.org) during 2026. This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. The small grey numbers are the pages of Kühn's Greek edition in books 1-5 and the entry numbers (book.chapter.entry) in books 6-11. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks; those checks are automated agreement tests, not a reading of the Greek, so a passage without the rule is not checked either. Words in square brackets were supplied by the model for the sense; Greek in parentheses gives the original word; any remark about a doubtful or corrupt reading is the model's, not a scholar's.

Galen, De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus (On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs), Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377, books 1-11

Greek: First1KGreek tlg0057.tlg075.1st1K-grc1 (Kühn 1826, vols. 11-12; books 1-11), CC BY-SA 4.0. Rendering A: claude-fable-5-1; rendering B: claude-opus-5-5, built 2026-11-28. The two halves were drafted and checked as separate runs, each with its own judge draw and its own numbers. Books 1-5: same neutral prompt as the other works, no glossary, fresh context; batch API; README and data: simpl-med-1-5. Books 6-11: the same prompt plus a glossary of Galen's own drug and quality terms taken from books 1-5 (glossary.tsv in its folder), fresh context; Claude Code CLI, which adds a fixed preamble of its own to every prompt; README and data: simpl-med-6-11. Pre-registered thresholds: firstdrafts_v02_spec.md with the judge of firstdrafts_v03_judge_note.md. Terms: green = both renderings agree on the term (after normalisation), red = they differ, grey = one rendering reported. Hover a mark for the Greek lemma and the other rendering. Unit difficulty = punch-list density per 100 tokens from the terminology density map (tercile within each half).

Check (a): the English back-translated into Greek by a third model shares at least half of the unit's term lemmas with the source. Check (b): the two renderings agree on at least 70% of the unit's target terms. Check (c): a model judge, salted with 10 planted errors and 10 anchors it had to get right, finds at least 80% of the term renderings within the LSJ entry's senses, with at most 2 outside. A unit fails when it fails two of three.

Flags, under each passage: places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors. Sentence by sentence, each rendering is compared with the Greek for numbers, negations and words of more or less, and for the names of drugs and parts of the body (a name in the English with none in the Greek, or a name in the Greek with none in the English). These are word-matching tests: a flag often comes from a sentence pair that is aligned a little off, a paraphrase or a name the model kept in Greek, and a passage with no flag is not checked by them either. The flags play no part in the unit verdicts.

Greek (Kühn)
8.13.1 [α΄. Περὶ νάρδου στάχυος.] Νάρδου στάχυς θερμαίνει μὲν κατὰ τὴν τρίτην ἀπόστασιν, ξηραίνει δὲ κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν συμπληρουμένην. σύγκειται δὲ ἔκ τε στυφούσης αὐτάρκως οὐσίας καὶ δριμείας θερμῆς οὐ πολλῆς καί τινος ὑποπίκρου βραχείας. ἐκ τοιούτων δ’ ἡ ῥίζα συγκειμένη δυνάμεων καὶ πρὸς ἧπαρ καὶ πρὸς στόμαχον εὐλόγως ἁρμόζει. πινομένη τε καὶ ἔξωθεν ἐπιτιθεμένη καὶ οὖρα κινεῖ καὶ δήξεις ἰᾶται στομάχου καὶ τὰ κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα καὶ τὰ ἔντερα ῥεύματα ξηραίνει καὶ πρὸς τούτοις ἔτι τὰ κατὰ τὴν κεφαλὴν καὶ τὸν θώρακα. ἰσχυροτέρα δ’ ἐστὶν ἡ Ἰνδικὴ προσαγορευομένη, μελαντέρα τῆς Συριακῆς καλουμένης ὑπάρχουσα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.13.1 [1. On spike of nard.] Spike of nard heats in the third degree, and dries in the second degree when it is complete. It is composed of a sufficiently astringent substance, of a pungent, hot substance that is not abundant, and of a certain slightly bitter one in small measure. Being composed of such powers, the root is reasonably suited both to the liver and to the stomach. Both when drunk and when applied externally it moves urine, heals bitings of the stomach, dries the fluxes in the belly and the intestines and, in addition to these, those in the head and the chest. The kind called Indian is stronger, being blacker than the kind called Syrian.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συμπληρουμένην, ἀπόστασιν
8.13.1 [1. On the spike of nard.] The spike of nard heats in the third degree, and dries in the second degree, at its completion. It is composed of a substance that is adequately astringent, of a pungent and hot one that is not abundant, and of a certain slightly bitter one in small amount. The root, being composed of such powers, is with good reason suited both to the liver and to the stomach. Both when drunk and when applied externally it moves urine, heals bitings of the stomach, and dries the fluxes in the belly and the intestines, and in addition to these also those in the head and the chest. Stronger is the kind called Indian, which is blacker than the one called Syrian.

Περὶ νάρθηκος.] Νάρθηκος τὸ μὲν σπέρμα θερμαίνει καὶ λεπτύνει, τὸ δ’ ἐντὸς ἔτι χλωρὸν, τὸ λεγόμενον ἐντεριώνη, στυπτικῆς τινος μετέχει ποιότητος, δι’ ἣν αἱμοπτυϊκοῖς τε καὶ κοιλιακοῖς ἁρμόττει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πάρδου
8.13.3 [3. On mountain nard.] Mountain nard, which is also called thylakitis and pyritis, grows most abundantly in Cilicia, and is weaker than those mentioned before. 8.13.4 [4. On narthex (giant fennel).] The seed of narthex (giant fennel) heats and thins, while the inner part, when still green, the so-called pith (enteriōnē), partakes of a certain astringent quality, on account of which it is suited to those who spit blood and to those with bowel complaints.

8.13.3 [3. On mountain nard.] Mountain nard, which is also called thylakitis and pyritis, grows in greatest abundance in Cilicia, and it is weaker than those mentioned before. 8.13.4 [4. On narthex.] Of the narthex, the seed heats and thins, while the inner part when still green, the so-called pith (enteriōnē), shares in a certain astringent quality, on account of which it is suitable for those who spit blood and for coeliacs.

8.13.7 [7. On neuras.] Neuras, which some call poterion, is of a drying power that does not bite, so that it is even believed to glue together severed sinews. Its roots especially are of this sort. And indeed some also give the decoction of them to those who suffer in the sinews.

8.13.7 [7. On neuras.] Neuras, which some call poterion, is of a drying power that does not bite, so that it is even believed to unite severed nerves. Its roots are especially of this kind. And indeed some also give the decoction of them to those who have suffered an affection of the nerves.

8.13.8 [η΄. Περὶ νηρίου.] Νήριον, ἢ ῥοδοδάφνη, γνώριμος ἅπασι θάμνος. ἔξωθεν μὲν τοῦ σώματος εἰ καταπλασθείη, διαφορητικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως, εἴσω δὲ λαμβανομένη ὀλέθριός τε καὶ δηλητήριος οὐκ ἀνθρώποις μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς πλείστοις τῶν βοσκημάτων.

8.13.8 [8. On nerion.] Nerion, or rhododaphne, is a shrub familiar to everyone. If it is applied as a plaster on the outside of the body, it is of a dispersing power; but taken internally it is destructive and deleterious, not only to human beings but also to most grazing animals.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
8.13.8 [8. On nērion.] Nērion, or rhododaphnē, is a shrub familiar to everyone. If it is applied as a cataplasm on the outside of the body, it is of a dispersing power; but taken internally it is deadly and deleterious not to human beings only, but also to most grazing animals.

Simp.8.13.9Simp. 8.13.9, ch. 8.13 difficulty mid (3.85/100; thin 1, absent 3) (a) pass (b) fail 5/8 (c) fail 13/16 unit: fail
Greek (Kühn)
8.13.9 [θ΄. Περὶ νυμφαίας.] Νυμφαίας ἥ τε ῥίζα καὶ τὸ σπέρμα δύναμιν ἔχει ξηραντικὴν ἄδηκτον. ἐπέχει τοιγαροῦν καὶ τὰ τῆς γαστρὸς ῥεύματα καὶ σπέρμα κατά τε τοὺς ὀνειρωγμοὺς καὶ ἄλλως ἀμετρότερον φερόμενον, ὀνίνησι δὲ καὶ δυσεντερικούς. ἡ δὲ τὴν λευκὴν ἔχουσα ῥίζαν νυμφαία σφοδροτέρας ἐστὶ δυνάμεως, ὥστε ῥοῦν γυναικεῖον ἰᾶσθαι. πίνεται δὲ καὶ αὕτη καὶ ἡ τὴν μέλαιναν ἔχουσα ῥίζαν ἐν οἴνῳ μέλανι αὐστηρῷ. μετέχουσι δέ τι καὶ ῥυπτικῆς δυνάμεως, ὥστε καὶ ἀλφοὺς ἰῶνται καὶ ἀλωπεκίας. ἀναδεύονται δὲ πρὸς μὲν τοὺς ἀλφοὺς ὕδατι, πρὸς δὲ τὰς ἀλωπεκίας ὑγρᾷ πίττῃ. κρείτ των δ’ εἰς ταῦτα ἡ τὴν μέλαιναν ἔχουσα ῥίζαν, ὥσπερ ἐς θάτερα ἡ τὴν λευκήν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.13.9 [9. On nymphaia.] Of nymphaia both the root and the seed have a drying power that does not bite. Accordingly it checks both the fluxes of the belly and seed that is carried off in nocturnal emissions and otherwise too immoderately, and it also benefits dysenterics. The nymphaia that has the white root is of a more vigorous power, so as to cure the female flux. Both this one and the one that has the black root are drunk in harsh black wine. They also partake somewhat of a cleansing power, so that they cure alphoi too, and alopeciai. They are soaked, for alphoi in water, for alopeciai in liquid pitch. The one that has the black root is better for these purposes, just as the one with the white root is for the other.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναδεύονται, ἐς θάτερα, ὀνειρωγμοὺς
8.13.9 [9. On nymphaea.] Both the root and the seed of nymphaea have a drying, non-biting power. Accordingly, they check fluxes of the belly, and also semen that is discharged in dream-emissions or otherwise too immoderately; and they also benefit dysenterics. The nymphaea that has the white root is of a more vehement power, so that it heals female flux. Both this one and the one that has the black root are drunk in dark, harsh wine. They also share to some degree in a cleansing power, so that they heal both alphoi and alopeciae. For the alphoi they are moistened with water, and for the alopeciae with liquid pitch. For these purposes the one that has the black root is better, just as the one with the white root is better for the other purposes.

8.15.10 [10. On vinegar.] Vinegar has been shown in the first of these commentaries to be of a mixed substance, cold and hot, both fine-particled; but the cold prevails over the hot. The drug is considerably drying, so as to be of the third order, already being completed, whenever it is strong.

8.15.11 [ια΄. Περὶ ὀξυακάνθου.] Ὀξυάκανθος. τοῦτο γὰρ τὸ δένδρον, ὥσπερ ἀχράδι παραπλήσιόν ἐστι τὴν ἰδέαν, οὕτω καὶ τὴν δύναμιν. καὶ μέν γε καὶ οἱ καρποὶ, ὁ μὲν τῆς ἀχράδος ἁπλῶς στρυφνὸς, ὁ δὲ τῆς ὀξυακάνθης καὶ λεπτομερής ἐστι καὶ τμητικὸν ὀλίγον ἔχει. ὁ δὲ καρπὸς τοῦ δένδρου τῷ μὲν τῆς ἀχράδος οὐκ ἔοικεν, ὅμοιος δ’ ἐστὶ τοῖς μύρτοις, ἐρυθρός τε καὶ ἀραιὸς ὑπάρχων. ἔχει δὲ καὶ πυρῆνας. οὐ μόνον δ’ ἐσθιόμενος, ἀλλὰ καὶ πινόμενος ἐφεκτικός ἐστιν ἁπάντων τῶν ῥοωδῶν παθῶν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀραιὸς, καρποὶ
8.15.11 [11. On oxyakanthos.] Oxyakanthos. For this tree, just as it is closely similar to the wild pear in appearance, so too is it in its power. And indeed the fruits as well: that of the wild pear is simply acerbic, whereas that of the oxyakanthē is also fine-particled and has a little that is cutting. The fruit of the tree, however, does not resemble that of the wild pear, but is similar to myrtle berries, being red and loose-textured. It also has stones. And not only when eaten but also when drunk it is checking of all fluxive affections.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γὰρ, καρποὶ, ἀραιὸς, ῥοωδῶν, ἐφεκτικός

8.15.12 [ιβ΄. Περὶ ὀποῦ.] Ὀπὸς, ὁ μὲν Κυρηναῖος ἁπάντων ἐστὶ θερμότατός τε καὶ λεπτομερέστατος καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ διαφορητικώτατος. οὐ μὴν ἀλλὰ καὶ οἱ ἄλλοι θερμοί τ’ εἰσὶν ἱκανῶς καὶ πνευματώδεις. τὸ πλεῖστον γὰρ ἐν αὐτοῖς ἀερώδους τε καὶ πυρώδους οὐσίας ἐστὶν. εἰσὶ δὲ πάμπολλοι μὲν ὀποί. ῥίζης γὰρ ἡστινοσοῦν ἢ καυλοῦ τμηθέντος τὸ ῥέον ἐκ τῆς τομῆς παχὺ καὶ γλίσχρον ὀπός ἐστιν. εἰδικώτερον δὲ οἷον καθ’ ὑπεροχήν τινα τὸν Κυρηναῖον οὕτως ὀνομάζουσιν καὶ τὸν Μηδικὸν καὶ τὸν Συριακόν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.15.12 [12. On juice.] Juice: the Cyrenaic is of all the hottest and the most fine-particled, and for this reason also the most dispersing. Nonetheless the others too are sufficiently hot and pneumatic; for the greater part in them is of airy and fiery substance. Juices are indeed very many in number: for when any root whatsoever or a stalk is cut, what flows from the incision, thick and viscous, is juice. But in a more specific sense, as it were by virtue of a certain preeminence, they give this name to the Cyrenaic, the Median and the Syrian.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πνευματώδεις
8.15.12 [12. On sap.] Sap: the Cyrenaic is the hottest and most fine-particled of all, and for this reason also the most dispersing. Nevertheless the others too are considerably hot and windy, for the greater part in them is of airy and fiery substance. There are very many saps, for when any root whatever or stem has been cut, what flows from the cut, thick and viscous, is sap. But more specifically, as by a kind of pre-eminence, they give this name to the Cyrenaic, the Median and the Syrian.

8.15.17 [ιζ΄. Περὶ ὄρχεως τοῦ κυνός.] Ὄρχις. ὀνομάζεται καὶ κυνὸς ὄρχις ἡ αὐτὴ βοτάνη. δύναμις δὲ τῆς ῥίζης αὐτοῦ βολβοειδοῦς οὔσης καὶ διπλῆς ὑγρὰ καὶ θερμή. διὸ καὶ γευομένοις γλυκύτερος φαίνεται. ἀλλ’ ἡ μὲν μείζων ῥίζα πολλὴν ἔοικεν ἔχειν ὑγρότητα περιττωματικήν τε καὶ φυσώδη, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ πρὸς ἀφροδίσια πινομένη προτρέπει. ἡ δ’ ἑτέρα ἡ ἐλάττων ἔμπαλιν κατειργασμένη ἱκανῶς, ὡς εἶναι τὴν κρᾶσιν αὐτῆς ἐπὶ τὸ θερμότερόν τε καὶ ξηρότερον ῥέπουσαν, ὅθεν οὐ μόνον οὐ προτρέπει τὰς πρὸς συνουσίας ὁρμὰς ἥδε ἡ ῥίζα, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοὐναντίον ἅπαν ἐπέχει τε καὶ καταστέλλει. ἐσθίονται δὲ δίκην βολβῶν ὀπτώμεναι.

8.15.17 [17. On dog's orchis.] Orchis. The same plant is also called dog's orchis (kynos orchis). The power of its root, which is bulb-like and double, is moist and hot; for this reason also it appears rather sweet to those who taste it. But the larger root seems to contain much moisture that is superfluous and flatulent, and on account of this, when drunk, it also incites to sexual intercourse (aphrodisia). The other, the smaller one, is on the contrary sufficiently worked up, so that its mixture inclines toward the hotter and drier; whence this root not only does not incite the impulses toward intercourse, but, entirely to the contrary, both checks and suppresses them. They are eaten roasted, in the manner of bulbs.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατειργασμένη
8.15.17 [17. On dog's orchis.] Orchis. The same plant is also called dog's orchis (kynos orchis). The power of its root, which is bulbous and double, is moist and hot; hence it also appears rather sweet to those who taste it. But the larger root seems to contain much moisture, both residual and flatulent; for this reason, when drunk, it also incites to sexual activity. The other, smaller one, conversely, is sufficiently worked up (concocted), so that its mixture inclines towards the hotter and drier; whence this root not only does not incite the impulses towards sexual intercourse, but, quite the contrary, actually checks and suppresses them. They are eaten roasted, in the manner of bulbs.

Περὶ ὄρχεως τοῦ σεραπιάδος.] Ὄρχις, ὃν καὶ σεραπιάδα καλοῦσιν, ξηροτέρας δυνάμεώς ἐστιν ἢ κατὰ τὴν προτέραν, ὅθεν εἰς τὰ ἀφροδίσια μὲν οὐχ ὁμοίως ἐπιτήδειός ἐστιν. οἰδήματα δὲ καταπλασσόμενος διαφορεῖ καὶ ἕλκη ῥυπαρὰ καθαίρει καὶ ἕρπητας ἰᾶται. ξηρανθεῖσα δὲ πολὺ δὴ μᾶλλον ἔτι ξηραίνει, ὥστε καὶ τὰ σηπεδονώδη καὶ τὰ κακοήθη τῶν ἑλκῶν ἰᾶται. καὶ γάρ τι καὶ ὑποστῦφον ἔχει καὶ διὰ τοῦτο κοιλίαν ἐπέχει μετ’ οἴνου πινομένη.

8.15.18 [18. On the orchis called serapias.] Orchis, which they also call serapias, is of a drier power than the former one, whence it is not similarly suitable for sexual intercourse. Applied as a plaster it disperses oedemas and cleanses filthy ulcers and heals herpetes. When dried it dries much more still, so that it heals both the putrefactive and the malignant among ulcers. For it also has something somewhat astringent, and on account of this it restrains the belly when drunk with wine.

8.15.18 [18. On the orchis [called] serapias.] Orchis, which they also call serapias, is of a drier power than the former, so that it is not equally suitable for sexual matters. 8.16.2 [2. On paliouros.] The leaves and the root of paliouros have a share, not obscurely, in an astringent power, so as even to check a flowing belly, and in a dispersing power to such a degree as even to treat phymata, at least those that are not excessively inflammatory and hot. 8.16.3 [γ΄. Περὶ παπύρου.] Πάπυρος αὐτὴ μὲν καθ’ ἑαυτὴν οὐδ’ εἰς χρείαν ἰατρικὴν ἥκει. βραχεῖσα δὲ ἢ καυθεῖσα χρησίμη γίνεται. ὀξυκράτῳ μὲν οὖν ἢ οἴνῳ βραχεῖσα κολλᾷ τὰ πρόσφατα τῶν ἑλκῶν καὶ μάλιστα ἐν κύκλῳ περικειμένη. ἀλλ’ ἐνταῦθα μὲν οἷον ὕλη τίς ἐστι δεκτικὴ τῶν ἰωμένων φαρμάκων. ἐπειδὰν δὲ καυθῇ, φάρμακον ἤδη γίνεται ξηραντικὸν, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡ τέφρα τοῦ κεκαυμένου χάρτου, πλὴν ὅσον ἀσθενεστέρα ἐστὶν ἡ τῆς παπύρου.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.16.3 [3. On papyrus.] Papyrus by itself does not come into medical use at all. But when soaked or burnt it becomes useful. Soaked, then, in oxykraton (vinegar-and-water) or in wine, it glues together fresh wounds, and especially when it is laid around them in a circle. But here it is, as it were, a kind of material receptive of the healing drugs. When, however, it has been burnt, it now becomes a drying drug, just as the ash of burnt paper also does, except in so far as that of papyrus is weaker.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χάρτου, ἰωμένων
8.16.3 [3. On papyrus.] Papyrus by itself, on its own, does not even come into medical use; but when soaked or burnt it becomes useful. Soaked, then, in oxycrate or wine, it agglutinates fresh wounds, especially when laid round them in a circle. But in this case it is, as it were, a kind of material receptive of the healing drugs. Once it has been burnt, however, it then becomes a drying drug, just as the ash of burnt paper also is, except in so far as that of papyrus is weaker.

Περὶ πάνακος τοῦ Ἡρακλείου.] Πάνακες Ἡράκλειον· ἐκ τούτου καὶ ὁ καλούμενος ὀποπάναξ γίνεται τῶν ῥιζῶν αὐτοῦ καὶ τῶν καυλῶν ἐκτεμνομένων. ἔστι δὲ ὁ μὲν ὀποπάναξ αὐτὸς πολυχρηστότατος, θερμαίνων καὶ μαλάττων καὶ διαφορῶν. καὶ θείη ἄν τις αὐτὸν κατὰ μὲν τὸ θερμαίνειν ἐκ τῆς τρίτης τάξεως, κατὰ δὲ τὸ ξηραίνειν ἐκ τῆς δευτέρας. ὁ δὲ φλοιὸς τῆς ῥίζης ξηραντικόν τε ἅμα καὶ θερμαντικόν ἐστι φάρμακον, ἀλλ’ ἧττον τοῦ ὀποῦ. καὶ μέντοι τι καὶ ῥυπτικὸν ἔχει. διὸ καὶ χρώμεθ’ αὐτῷ πρός τε τὰ γυμνὰ τῶν ὀστῶν καὶ τὰ κακοηθευόμενα τῶν ἑλκῶν. ἱκανῶς γάρ ἐστι τὰ τοιαῦτα σαρκοῦντα, κᾀν τῷ ῥύπτειν τε ἅμα καὶ ξηραίνειν καὶ μὴ πάνυ σφοδρῶς θερμαίνειν, ὧν ἁπάντων δεῖ τῷ μέλλοντι σαρκοῦν, ὡς ἐν τοῖς τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου γράμμασιν ἐπιδείκνυμεν. ὁ δὲ καρπὸς αὐτοῦ, θερμὸς ὢν καὶ αὐτὸς, ἐμμήνων ἀγωγόν ἐστι φάρμακον. εἴθισται δ’ οὐκ οἶδ’ ὅπως ἤδη σχεδὸν ἅπασιν οὐ πάνακες, ἀλλὰ πάνακα προσαγορεύειν τὴν πόαν ταύτην.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.16.4 [4. On the Heraclean panakes.] Heraclean panakes: from this is also produced what is called opopanax, when its roots and stalks are cut. The opopanax itself is of very manifold use, being heating, softening and dispersing. One would place it, as regards heating, in the third rank, and as regards drying, in the second. The bark of the root is a drying and at the same time heating drug, but less so than the juice; and indeed it also has something cleansing. For this reason we use it both for bones laid bare and for ulcers that are becoming malignant. For such things are sufficiently flesh-producing, in that they cleanse and at the same time dry and do not heat very violently, all of which are required of what is to produce flesh, as we demonstrate in the books of the Therapeutic Method. Its fruit, being itself also hot, is a drug promotive of the menses. It has become customary, I know not how, for almost everyone now to call this herb not panakes but panax.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκ τῆς τρίτης τάξεως, ἐκ τῆς δευτέρας, κᾀν τῷ ῥύπτειν, πάνακα
8.16.4 [4. On Heracleian panax.] Heracleian panakes: from this also comes what is called opopanax, when its roots and stalks are cut into. Opopanax itself is of very many uses, being heating, softening and dispersing. One might place it, as regards heating, in the third order, and as regards drying, in the second. The bark of the root is a drug that is at once drying and heating, but less so than the juice. It also has something cleansing about it. For this reason we use it both for bones that are laid bare and for ulcers that are becoming malignant. For it is quite capable of filling such things with flesh, in that it at once cleanses and dries and does not heat very vehemently, all of which is required of whatever is to fill with flesh, as we demonstrate in the books of the Therapeutic Method. Its fruit, being itself also hot, is a drug that is a promoter of the menses. But, I do not know how, it has now become customary with almost everyone to call this herb not panakes but panax (πάνακα).

8.16.6 [6. On Chironian panakes.] Chironian panakes. This too is of a power similar to the one written before. 8.16.7 [7. On paronychia.] Paronychia has been named from its action, for it cures paronychias; and, as Dioscorides says, also kēria. Its power is fine-particled and drying and does not bite. For such too must the drugs for paronychia be. And it is clear that a drug of this kind is also capable of dispersing all the affections that need to be dispersed. 8.16.8 [8. On pentaphyllon.] Pentaphyllon (cinquefoil). The root dries strongly, but is least of all pungent. Περὶ πεπέρεως.] Πεπέρεως ἡ μὲν ῥίζα κόστῳ μάλιστα κατὰ τὴν δύναμιν ἔοικεν. ὁ δὲ καρπὸς ὁ μὲν ἄρτι βλαστάνοντος αὐτοῦ τὸ μακρόν ἐστι πέπερι, διὸ καὶ ὑγρότερον ὑπάρχει τοῦ πεπείρου. σημεῖον δὲ τῆς ὑγρότητος αὐτοῦ τό τε ῥᾳδίως ἀποτιθέμενον τιτρῶσθαι καὶ τὸ μὴ παραχρῆμα δάκνειν, ἀλλὰ μετ’ ὀλίγον μὲν ἄρχεσθαι, παραμένειν δὲ ἐπιπλέον. ὁ δ’ οἷον ὄμφαξ καρπὸς τὸ λευκόν ἐστι πέπερι, δριμύτερον ὑπάρχον τοῦ μέλανος. ἐκεῖνο γὰρ οἷον ὑπερωπτημένον ἐστὶν ἤδη καὶ ὑπερεξηραμμένον. ἀμφότερα δὲ ἰσχυρῶς θερμαίνει τε καὶ ξηραίνει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τιτρῶσθαι
8.16.11 [11. On pepper.] Of pepper, the root resembles costus most closely in its power. As for the fruit, that of the plant when it has only just sprouted is long pepper, and for this reason it is also moister than the ripe fruit. A sign of its moistness is both that, when stored away, it is easily bored through, and that it does not bite immediately, but begins after a short while and then persists for a longer time. The fruit that is, as it were, in its unripe-grape stage is white pepper, which is more pungent than the black; for the latter is already, so to speak, over-roasted and over-dried. Both heat and dry strongly.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοῦ πεπείρου (taken as 'the ripe' fruit, from πέπειρος; there may be a play on πέπερι, or the text may be corrupt), ἀποτιθέμενον τιτρῶσθαι (taken as 'when stored it is readily pierced', i.e. bored by worms; the exact sense of τιτρῶσθαι here is uncertain)
8.16.11 [11. On pepper.] The root of pepper most resembles costus in its power. Of the fruit, that of the plant when it is just sprouting is long pepper, which is why it is also moister than the ripe fruit. A sign of its moisture is both that, when stored, it is readily pierced, and that it does not bite at once, but begins to do so after a short while and persists for a longer time. The fruit that is like an unripe grape is white pepper, which is more pungent than the black. For the black is, as it were, already over-roasted and over-dried. Both heat and dry strongly.

8.16.12 [ιβ΄. Περὶ περσέας.] Περσέας τὰ φύλλα στυπτικῆς μετείληφε συμμέτρως δυνάμεως, ὡς δύνασθαί ποτε καὶ τοῖς αἱμοῤῥαγοῦσι μορίοις ἐπιτίθεσθαι συμφερόντως. 8.16.13 [ιγ΄. Περὶ περικλυμένου.] Περικλυμένου χρήσιμος ὅ τε καρπός ἐστι καὶ τὰ φύλλα, τμητικῆς τε ἅμα καὶ θερμαινούσης ὑπάρχοντα δυνάμεως εἰς τοσοῦτον, ὥστε καὶ τὸ οὖρον αἱματῶδες ἐργάζεσθαι, πινόμενα μέχρι πλείονος. ἐν ἀρχῇ μέντοι οὐρητικὸν μόνον ἐστὶν, ἔξωθεν δὲ μετ’ ἐλαίου συναλειφόμενον θερμαίνει. ὠφελεῖ δὲ καὶ σπληνώδεις καὶ δυσπνοοῦντας. ἡ δὲ συμμετρία τῆς πόσεως δραχμῆς μιᾶς πλῆθος σὺν οἴνῳ. ξηραίνει δὲ καὶ σπέρμα, καί τινές φασιν εἰ ἐπὶ πλέον πίνοιτο, παντάπασιν ἀγόνους γίνεσθαι τοὺς πίνοντας. ὁρίζουσι δ’ ἔνιοι καὶ προθεσμίαν ἡμερῶν ἐπὶ τῇ τοιαύτῃ πόσει, καθάπερ καὶ Διοσκορίδης ἑπτὰ καὶ τριάκοντα λέγων ἱστορεῖσθαι αὐτάς. οὗτος δὲ καὶ τὸ οὖρον αἱματῶδες γίνεσθαί φησιν ἀπὸ τῆς ἕκτης τῶν ἡμερῶν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἱστορεῖσθαι
8.16.12 [12. On persea.] The leaves of persea partake moderately of an astringent power, so that they can at times also be applied beneficially to parts that are haemorrhaging. 8.16.13 [13. On periclymenon.] Of periclymenon both the fruit and the leaves are useful, being of a cutting and at the same time heating power to such a degree that they even render the urine bloody when drunk for a longer period. At the beginning, however, it is only diuretic, while applied externally in an ointment with oil it heats. It also benefits those suffering from the spleen and those with difficulty in breathing. The proper measure of the draught is the quantity of one drachm with wine. It also dries up the seed, and some say that, if it is drunk for a longer time, those who drink it become altogether infertile. Some also fix an appointed term of days for such a draught, as Dioscorides too does, saying that these are recorded as thirty-seven. He also says that the urine becomes bloody from the sixth of the days.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἱστορεῖσθαι, σπληνώδεις

8.16.16 [ιστ΄. Περὶ πετροσελίνου.] Πετροσελίνου τὸ σπέρμα μάλιστ’ ἐστὶ χρήσιμον, οὔσης καὶ ὅλης τῆς πόας ἅμα ταῖς ῥίζαις ὁμοίας μὲν, ἀσθενεστέρας δὲ δυνάμεως. ἔστι δ’ ὥσπερ ἐν τῇ γεύσει δριμὺ μετὰ πικρότητος, οὕτω κᾀν τοῖς ἔργοις θερμὸν ἅμα τῷ τέμνειν, καὶ διὰ ταῦτα καταμήνιά τε καὶ οὖρα προτρέπει δαψιλῶς. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἄφυσον. εἴη ἂν οὖν καὶ αὐτὸ τῆς τρίτης τάξεως τῶν θερμαινόντων τε καὶ ξηραινόντων. οἱ δ’ ἐν Κιλικίᾳ πετροσέλινον μόνον ὀνομάζουσιν τὸ ἐν τῷ Ἀμάνῳ γεννώμενον. ἔστι δὲ σμύρνιον μᾶλλον ἢ πετροσέλινον, οὐ μὴν παραλλάττει γε πολὺ τὴν πετροσελίνου δύναμιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.16.16 [16. On parsley.] Of parsley the seed is the most useful part, the whole herb together with the roots being of a similar but weaker power. And just as in taste it is pungent with bitterness, so also in its actions it is heating together with cutting, and for these reasons it promotes the menses and urine abundantly. It also dispels flatulence. It too, then, would be of the third order of things that heat and dry. Those in Cilicia call parsley only that which grows on Amanus; but this is smyrnion rather than parsley, though it certainly does not depart much from the power of parsley.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)

It is also free of flatulence. It would therefore itself also belong to the third order of things that heat and dry. The people in Cilicia call 'parsley' only the plant that grows on the Amanus. It is, however, smyrnion rather than parsley, yet it certainly does not differ much from the power of parsley.

Greek (Kühn)
8.16.17 [ιζ΄. Περὶ πευκεδάνου.] Πευκεδάνου τῇ ῥίζῃ μὲν μάλιστα, χρώμεθα δὲ καὶ τῷ ὀπῷ καὶ τῷ χυλῷ. ἔστι δὲ πάντα μὲν ταῦτα τῆς αὐτῆς τῷ εἴδει δυνάμεως. ἰσχυρότερος δ’ αὐτῶν ὁ ὀπὸς ἱκανῶς θερμαίνων καὶ διαφορῶν, ὅθεν καὶ τοῖς κατὰ τὰ νεῦρα πάθεσιν ἅπασιν ἁρμόττειν πεπίστευται. καὶ τὰ κατὰ πνεύμονά τε καὶ θώρακα νοσήματα τὰ διὰ πάχος ἢ γλισχρότητα χυμῶν γινόμενα. καὶ εἴσω μὲν τοῦ σώματος λαμβανόμενος, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὀσμώμενος ὠφελεῖ. τῷ δὲ λεπτύνειν καὶ τέμνειν καὶ ὀδόντων τετρημένων ἐνίοτε παραχρῆμα τὰς ὀδύνας ἔπαυσεν, ἐντεθεὶς τῷ βρώματι, διότι λεπτομερής τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ θερμαντικός. καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ σπλῆνας ἐσκιῤῥωμένους ὠφελεῖ τῷ τέμνειν καὶ διαφορεῖν καὶ λεπτύνειν τοὺς παχεῖς χυμούς. τῇ δὲ ῥίζῃ πρὸς ταῦτα μὲν ἔστι χρῆσθαι. καὶ λεπίδας δὲ ὀστῶν ἀφίστησι τάχιστα, διότι ξηραίνει σφοδρῶς, ἔλαττον δὲ ἢ κατὰ τὸν ὀπὸν θερμαίνουσα. καὶ τοῖς κακοήθεσιν ἕλκεσιν ἄριστόν ἐστι φάρμακον ἐπιπλαττομένη ξηρά. αὐτὴ γὰρ καθαίρει καὶ σαρκοῖ καὶ μέχρι τῶν ἐπουλώσεων ἄγει, θερμαίνουσα μὲν κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν τάξιν ἤδη που συμπληρουμένην, ξηραίνουσα δὲ κατὰ τὴν τρίτην ἀρχομένην.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βρώματι, χυλῷ
8.16.17 [17. On peucedanum.] Of peucedanum we use chiefly the root, but we use also the juice and the expressed juice. All of these are of the same power in kind; but the strongest of them is the juice, which heats and disperses considerably, whence it has also been believed to be suitable for all the affections of the sinews, and for the diseases of the lung and chest that arise through thickness or viscosity of humours. It benefits both when taken into the body and also when smelled. And by its thinning and cutting it has sometimes stopped at once the pains of hollowed teeth, when inserted into the cavity, because it is fine-particled and heating. And indeed it also benefits indurated spleens by cutting and dispersing and thinning the thick humours. The root may be used for these same purposes; and it also detaches scales of bones very quickly, because it dries vigorously, though it heats less than the juice. For malignant ulcers it is an excellent drug when plastered on dry; for it both cleanses and produces flesh and brings them as far as cicatrization, heating in the second order, already more or less completed, and drying in the third, at its beginning.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: βρώματι (taken as the carious cavity of the tooth rather than 'food'), τὰ κατὰ πνεύμονά τε καὶ θώρακα νοσήματα (accusative without a clear governing verb; construed with ἁρμόττειν), ἤδη που συμπληρουμένην
8.16.17 [17. On peucedanum.] Of peucedanum we use the root above all, but we also use the sap and the juice. All of these are of the same power in kind, but the sap is the strongest of them, heating and dispersing considerably; for this reason it is also believed to be suited to all affections of the nerves, and to the diseases of the lung and thorax that arise from thickness or viscosity of the humours. It is beneficial not only when taken inside the body, but also when smelled. By thinning and cutting it has sometimes immediately stopped the pains of perforated teeth when placed in the carious cavity, because it is fine-particled and heating. Moreover, it also benefits indurated (scirrhous) spleens by cutting, dispersing and thinning the thick humours. The root may be used for these purposes as well. It also detaches scales of bone very quickly, because it dries vehemently, while heating less than the sap does. And for malignant ulcers it is an excellent drug when plastered on dry. For it cleanses, fills with flesh and leads on to cicatrisation, heating in the second order where this is by now somewhere near completion, and drying in the third at its beginning.

8.16.18 [ιη΄. Περὶ πηγάνου.] Πήγανον τὸ μὲν ἄγριον ἐκ τῆς τετάρτης ἐστὶν ἤδη τάξεως τῶν θερμαινόντων, τὸ δ’ ἥμερον ἐκ τῆς τρίτης. ἔστι δ’ οὐ μόνον δριμὺ γευόμενον, ἀλλὰ καὶ πικρὸν, ᾧ καὶ τὸ τέμνειν τε καὶ διαφορεῖν ἔχει τοὺς παχεῖς καὶ γλίσχρους χυμούς. διὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν δύναμιν καὶ δι’ οὔρων κινεῖ. καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ λεπτομερές ἐστι καὶ ἄφυσον, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πρός τε τὰς ἐμπνευματώσεις ἁρμόττει καὶ τὰς πρὸς ἀφροδίσια προθυμίας ἐπέχει καὶ διαφορεῖ καὶ ξηραίνει γενναίως. ἔστι γὰρ τῶν ἰσχυρῶς ξηραινόντων φαρμάκων. ὃ δ’ ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν λόγοις μῶλυ καὶ βήσασαν ἔφαμεν ὀνομάζεσθαι, εἴη ἂν καὶ αὐτὸ πήγανον ἄγριον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.16.18 [18. On rue.] Rue: the wild kind is already of the fourth order of heating agents, the cultivated kind of the third. It is not only pungent to the taste but also bitter, by which it also has the power of cutting and dispersing thick and viscous humours. By the same power it also moves through the urine. Moreover, it is also fine-particled and dispels flatulence, and for this reason it is suited to flatulent distensions, and it checks the urges towards sexual intercourse, and it disperses and dries vigorously; for it is among the drugs that dry strongly. And what in the preceding discussions we said is called moly and besasa would itself also be wild rue.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: βήσασαν, δι’ οὔρων κινεῖ
8.16.18 [18. On rue.] Rue: the wild kind already belongs to the fourth order of heating things, the cultivated kind to the third. To the taste it is not only pungent but also bitter, and by virtue of this it also has the property of cutting and dispersing thick and viscid humours. Through this same power it also provokes evacuation by way of the urine. Moreover, it is also fine-particled and free of flatulence, and for this reason it is suited to inflations, checks the desires for sexual intercourse, and disperses and dries vigorously; for it is one of the strongly drying drugs. And that which in the earlier discussions we said is called moly and besasa would itself also be wild rue.

8.16.19 [ιθ΄. Περὶ πίσσης.] Πίσσα ἡ μὲν ξηρὰ ξηραίνει καὶ θερμαίνει κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν ἀπόστασιν ἀπὸ τῶν συμμέτρων καὶ πλέον γε ξηραίνειν πέφυκεν ἢ θερμαίνειν. ἡ δ’ ὑγρὰ τοὔμπαλιν θερμαίνει πλέον ἢ ξηραίνει. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ λεπτομερὲς, ᾧ καὶ τοὺς ἀσθματικοὺς καὶ τοὺς ἐμπυϊκοὺς ὠφελεῖ. πλῆθος δ’ ἐκλαβεῖν ἀρκεῖ κύαθον μέλιτος μιγνύντας, ἀλλὰ καὶ ῥυπτικὸν ἔχουσί τι καὶ συμπεπτικὸν καὶ διαφορητικὸν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν ὑπόπικρόν τε καὶ δριμύ. οὕτως γέ τοι καὶ λεπροὺς ὄνυχας ἐξάγουσιν μιγνύμεναι κηρῷ καὶ λειχῆνας ἀποῤῥύπτουσι. συμπέπτουσι δὲ καὶ τοὺς σκληροὺς καὶ ἀπέπτους ὄγκους ἅπαντας ἐπεμβαλλόμεναι τοῖς καταπλάσμασιν, ἰσχυρότερα δ’ εἰς ἅπαντα τὰ τοιαῦτα ἡ ὑγρά. ἡ δὲ ξηρὰ πίττα πρὸς μὲν ταῦτα χείρων, εἰς δὲ τὰς κολλήσεις τῶν τραυμάτων ἐπιτηδειοτέρα. δῆλον οὖν ἐκ τούτων ὡς ὑγρότητος θερμῆς ἀναμέμικταί τι δαψιλὲς τῇ ὑγρᾷ πίττῃ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκλαβεῖν
8.16.19 [19. On pitch.] Pitch: the dry kind dries and heats at the second degree of removal from the moderate, and it is by nature more apt to dry than to heat; the liquid kind, conversely, heats more than it dries. It also has something fine-particled, by which it benefits both asthmatics and those suffering from empyema. As to quantity, it suffices to take a cyathus, mixing it with honey. But they also have something cleansing and concocting and dispersing, just as to the taste too they are slightly bitter and pungent. Thus indeed they also remove leprous nails when mixed with wax, and they cleanse away lichens. They also concoct all hard and unconcocted swellings when added to plasters, and for all such purposes the liquid kind is stronger. Dry pitch is inferior for these things, but more suitable for the gluing together of wounds. It is clear, then, from these things that a considerable amount of hot moisture is mixed into liquid pitch.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πλῆθος δ’ ἐκλαβεῖν ἀρκεῖ κύαθον μέλιτος μιγνύντας, ἐκλαβεῖν, κύαθον μέλιτος, λεπροὺς, ἐξάγουσιν
8.16.19 [19. On pitch.] Dry pitch dries and heats in the second remove from the moderate, and it is of a nature to dry more than to heat. Liquid pitch, conversely, heats more than it dries. It also has something fine-particled, by which it benefits the asthmatic and those with empyema. As for quantity, it is enough to take a cyathus, mixing it with honey [lit. 'a cyathus of honey']. But they also have something cleansing, concocting and dispersing, just as they are also somewhat bitter and pungent to the taste. Thus, mixed with wax, they remove leprous nails and cleanse away leichenes. They also concoct all hard and unconcocted swellings when added to poultices, and for all such purposes the liquid is stronger. Dry pitch is inferior for these purposes, but more suitable for the gluing together of wounds. It is clear from this, then, that an abundant quantity of hot moisture is mixed into liquid pitch.

Περὶ πίτυος.] Πίτυος ὁ φλοιὸς ἐπικρατοῦσαν ἔχει τὴν στυπτικὴν δύναμιν εἰς τοσοῦτον ὡς παρατρίμματά τε καταπλαττόμενα ἰᾶσθαι κάλλιστα καὶ κοιλίαν ἐπέχειν, εἰ ποθείη. καὶ τὰ κατακαύματα δὲ ἐπουλοῖ. καὶ ὁ τῆς πεύκης δὲ φλοιὸς ὅμοιος μὲν αὐτῷ, μετριώτερος δὲ κατὰ τὴν δύναμιν. ἐν δὲ τοῖς φύλλοις ἀμφοτέρων τῶν δένδρων ὡς ἂν ὑγροτέροις οὖσι πολὺ τοῦ φλοιοῦ δύναμίς ἐστι κολλητικὴ τραυμάτων. ἐν μέντοι τῷ κώνῳ, κᾂν εἰ παραπλησία τούτοις, ἀλλ’ ἰσχυροτέρα τε ἡ δύναμίς ἐστιν, ἔν τε τῷ φλοιῷ καὶ τοῖς φύλλοις, ὡς μηδὲν ἔτι τῶν προειρημένων καλῶς δύνασθαι ποιεῖν, ἀλλ’ ἤδη τι δακνῶδες ἔχειν ἀνιαρόν. ἡ δὲ λιγνὺς ἡ ἐκ τῶν εἰρημένων πρὸς πτίλα βλέφαρα καὶ μυδρῶντας κανθοὺς καὶ περιβεβρωμένους καὶ δακρύοντάς ἐστι χρήσιμος.

8.16.23 [23. On the pine (pitys).] The bark of the pine (pitys) has the astringent power prevailing in it, to such a degree that, applied as a plaster, it cures chafings (paratrimmata) excellently and checks the bowels, if it is drunk. It also cicatrizes burns. And the bark of the fir (peukē) is similar to it, but more moderate in power. In the leaves of both trees, since they are much moister than the bark, there is a power agglutinating of wounds. In the kōnos-pine, however, even if the power is similar to these, it is nevertheless stronger, both in the bark and in the leaves, so that it can no longer do well any of the things mentioned before, but already has something biting and distressing. The soot (lignys) from the aforesaid is useful for eyelids affected with ptilosis (ptila), for corners of the eyes that are running (mydrōntes), eroded around and weeping.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔν τε τῷ φλοιῷ καὶ τοῖς φύλλοις (the comparison 'than in the bark and the leaves' seems to need ἢ ἐν; text possibly corrupt), μυδρῶντας (probably for μυδῶντας, 'dank, moist with discharge'), εἰ ποθείη (taken as 'if drunk'), πεύκης (rendered 'peukē-pine' to distinguish it from πίτυς)
8.16.23 [23. On the pine (pitys).] The bark of the pine has the astringent power predominant to such a degree that, applied as a plaster, it heals chafings excellently, and it checks the belly if drunk. It also cicatrizes burns. The bark of the peukē-pine is similar to it, but more moderate in its power. In the leaves of both trees, inasmuch as they are much moister than the bark, there is a power agglutinating wounds. In the cone, however, even if the power is close to these, it is nevertheless stronger than in the bark and the leaves, so that it can no longer do any of the aforementioned things well, but already has something biting and distressing. The soot from the aforesaid is useful for eyelids that have lost their lashes, and for canthi that are dank, eroded round about, and watering.

8.16.25 [κε΄. Περὶ πλατάνου.]΄ Πλάτανος ὑγροτέρας ἐστὶ καὶ ψυχροτέρας οὐσίας οὐ πολλῷ τινι τῶν συμμέτρων. διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τὰ φύλλα τὰ χλωρὰ λειωθέντα καὶ καταπλασθέντα τὰς ἐν γενέσει φλεγμονὰς ὀνίνησιν οὐκ ἀσαφῶς. ὁ δὲ φλοιὸς αὐτοῦ καὶ τὰ σφαιρία ξηραντικωτέρας ἤδη δυνάμεώς ἐστιν, ὡς τὸν μὲν ἐν ὄξει καθεψόμενον εἰς ὀδόντων ἀλγήματα παραλαμβάνεσθαι, τὰ δὲ σφαιρία μετὰ στέατος ἐπὶ τῶν πυρικαύτων ἑλκῶν. εἰσὶ δ’ οἳ καὶ καύσαντες τὸν φλοιὸν ξηραντικόν τε καὶ ῥυπτικὸν ἀπεργάζονται φάρμακον, ὡς μεθ’ ὕδατος μὲν ἰᾶσθαι λέπρας, αὐτὸ δὲ καθ’ ἑαυτὸ ἐπιπλαττόμενον τὰ δι’ ὑγρότητα πολλὴν ἕλκη παλαιὰ καὶ ῥυπαρά. φυλάττεσθαι δὲ τὸν ἐπὶ τοῖς φύλλοις τοῦ δένδρου χνοῦν· καὶ γὰρ τὴν ἀρτηρίαν εἰσπνευσθεὶς ἀδικεῖ, ξηραίνων τε καὶ τραχύνων σφοδρῶς, καὶ κακοῖ τὴν φωνὴν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τὴν ὄψιν καὶ τὴν ἀκοὴν ὀφθαλμοῖς καὶ ὠσὶν ἐμπεσών.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.16.25 [25. On the plane tree.] The plane tree is of a moister and colder substance, not by any great margin, than the moderate ones. For this reason its green leaves, when pounded fine and applied as a plaster, give manifest benefit to inflammations in their beginning. Its bark and its little globes, however, are already of a more drying power, so that the former, boiled down in vinegar, is employed for pains of the teeth, and the little globes, with fat, for ulcers caused by burning. There are also some who, by burning the bark, produce a drying and cleansing drug, so that with water it cures lepra, and applied by itself as a plaster it cures old and foul ulcers which arise from much moisture. One should guard, however, against the down on the leaves of the tree; for when breathed in it injures the windpipe, drying and roughening it violently, and it damages the voice, just as it also damages the sight and the hearing when it falls into the eyes and ears.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πλατάνου.]΄, ἀρτηρίαν, χνοῦν
8.16.25 [25. On the plane tree.] The plane tree is of a moister and colder substance than the moderate ones, though not by very much. For this reason its green leaves, pounded smooth and applied as a poultice, benefit inflammations in the process of forming, and not obscurely. Its bark and its little balls are already of a more drying power, so that the bark, boiled down in vinegar, is employed for pains of the teeth, and the little balls, with fat, for ulcers caused by burning. There are some who also burn the bark and make of it a drying and cleansing drug, so that with water it heals leprai, and applied by itself as a plaster it heals ulcers that are old and foul through much moisture. One must beware of the down on the leaves of the tree: for when breathed in it harms the windpipe, drying and roughening it violently, and it damages the voice, just as it also damages sight and hearing when it falls into the eyes and ears.

Simp.8.16.26Simp. 8.16.26, ch. 8.16 difficulty high (5.66/100; thin 7, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
8.16.26 [κστ΄. Περὶ πολυγόνου.] Πολύγονον ἔχει μέν τι καὶ στῦφον, ἐπικρατεῖ δ’ ἐν αὐτῷ τὸ ὑδατῶδες ψυχρὸν, ὡς εἶναι κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν τάξιν ἐν τοῖς ψύχουσι φαρμάκοις, ἢ καὶ τῆς τρίτης που κατὰ τὴν ἀρχήν. ὀνίνησι γοῦν τοὺς ἐγκαιομένους τὸν στόμαχον ἔξωθεν ἐπιπλασσόμενον ψυχρὸν, ὥσπερ καὶ τὰ ἐρυσιπέλατα καὶ τὰς θερμὰς φλεγμονάς. ἅτε δὲ τοιοῦτον ὑπάρχον καὶ ἀποκρούεται ῥεύματα καὶ ταύτῃ δοκεῖ ξηραντικὸν ὑπάρχειν. διὸ καὶ τῶν ἑρπήτων ἀγαθόν ἐστιν φάρμακον, ἑλκῶν τε καὶ τῶν ἄλλων, ἐναργέστατα δὲ τῶν φλεγμονωδῶν τε καὶ ῥευματικῶν. ἔστι δὲ καὶ τῶν ἐναίμων τραυμάτων κολλητικόν. ὀνίνησι δὲ καὶ τὰ ἐν τοῖς ὠσὶν ἕλκη, κᾂν πῦον ἱκανὸν ἐν αὐτοῖς ᾖ, καὶ τοῦτο ξηραίνει. διὰ δὲ τὰς αὐτὰς δυνάμεις καὶ ῥοῦν γυναικεῖον ἵστησι καὶ δυσεντερίαν καὶ τὰς τοῦ αἵματος ἀναγωγὰς καὶ ὁποθενοῦν ἑτέρωθεν ἀμετροτέρας φοράς. φησὶ δὲ Διοσκορίδης καὶ οὖρα προκαλεῖσθαι αὐτὸ τοῖς στραγγουριῶσιν διδόμενον, οὐ μὴν διαιρεῖταί γε τὴν διάθεσιν ἀκριβῶς ἐφ’ ἧς χρὴ διδόναι. ἔστι δὲ εἰς ἅπαντα τὰ εἰρημένα τὸ ἄῤῥεν τοῦ θήλεος ἰσχυρότερον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐγκαιομένους τὸν στόμαχον, ἑρπήτων
8.16.26 [26. On polygonon (knotgrass).] Polygonon has something astringent in it too, but what prevails in it is the watery cold, so that it is in the second order among cooling drugs, or perhaps somewhere at the beginning of the third. At any rate it benefits those who suffer a burning heat of the stomach when applied cold from outside as a plaster, as it does also erysipelas and hot inflammations. And being of such a nature it also repels fluxes, and in this way it seems to be drying. For this reason it is also a good drug for herpetic sores (herpetes), and for ulcers, both the other kinds and most manifestly those that are inflamed and fluxional. It is also agglutinating for bleeding wounds. It benefits too the ulcers in the ears, and even if there is a considerable amount of pus in them, it dries this up as well. Through the same powers it also stops the female flux and dysentery and the bringing up of blood, and immoderate discharges from any other source whatever. Dioscorides says that it also provokes urine when given to those suffering from strangury, yet he does not define precisely the condition in which it ought to be given. For all the purposes mentioned the male is stronger than the female.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐγκαιομένους τὸν στόμαχον, ἀναγωγάς, φοράς
8.16.26 [26. On polygonon.] Polygonon has something astringent as well, but the watery cold predominates in it, so that it stands in the second order among cooling drugs, or perhaps even somewhere at the beginning of the third. At any rate, applied cold externally as a poultice, it benefits those who suffer burning in the stomach, as it also benefits erysipelas and hot inflammations. Being of such a kind, it also repels fluxes, and in this respect it seems to be drying. For this reason it is also a good drug for herpes, and for ulcers, both the others and, most evidently, those that are inflamed and fluxional. It is also agglutinating of bloody wounds. It also benefits ulcers in the ears, even if there is a good deal of pus in them, and this too it dries. Through the same powers it also stops female flux, dysentery, the bringing up of blood, and immoderate discharges from any other place whatever. Dioscorides also says that it provokes urine when given to those suffering from strangury; he does not, however, distinguish precisely the condition in which it ought to be given. For all the things mentioned the male is stronger than the female.

8.16.27 [κζ΄. Περὶ πολυγάλου.] Πολύγαλον αὐστηρὰ μετρίως ἔχει τὰ φύλλα. δοκεῖ δὲ πινόμενα γάλα γεννᾷν, ὥστ’ ἐπικρατοίη ἂν ἐν αὐτῷ τὸ θερμὸν καὶ ὑγρόν. 8.16.28 [κη΄. Περὶ πολυγονάτου.] Πολυγόνατον μικτῆς ἐστι δυνάμεώς τε καὶ ποιότητος. ἔχει γάρ τι καὶ στύψεως καὶ δριμύτητος καὶ πικρότητος καί τινος ἀηδίας ἀῤῥήτου. ταῦτ’ ἄρα οὐδὲ πολύχρηστόν ἐστιν ἀλλ’ ἢ μόνον ὅτι τὴν ῥίζαν ἐπιπλάττουσι τραύμασιν ἔνιοι. εἰσὶ δ’ οἳ τοὺς ἐν προσώπῳ σπίλους ἀποῤῥύπτουσιν δι’ αὐτοῦ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.16.27 [27. On polygalon.] Polygalon has leaves that are moderately harsh. When drunk they are thought to generate milk, so that the hot and the moist would predominate in it. 8.16.28 [28. On polygonaton.] Polygonaton is of a mixed power and quality; for it has something both of astringency and of pungency and of bitterness and of a certain indescribable unpleasantness. For these reasons, then, it is not even of manifold use, except only that some apply the root as a plaster to wounds. And there are those who cleanse away the spots on the face by means of it.

8.16.30 [30. On polion.] Polion is bitter to those who taste it and moderately pungent. 8.16.34 [λδ΄. Περὶ πολυποδίου.] Πολυπόδιον τὴν γλυκεῖαν ἅμα καὶ αὐστηρὰν ἐπικρατοῦσαν ἔχει ποιότητα, ὡς εἶναι δυνάμεως ξηραντικῆς ἱκανῶς ἀδήκτου. 8.16.35 [λε΄. Περὶ πρασίου.] Πράσιον ὥσπερ τῇ γεύσει πικρόν ἐστιν, οὕτω καὶ χρωμένοις ὁμολογοῦσαν ἔχει τῷ τοιούτῳ ﻿ χυμῷ τὴν ἐνέργειαν, ἐκφράττον καὶ ἧπαρ καὶ σπλῆνα καὶ τὰ κατὰ θώρακα καὶ πνεύμονα διακαθαῖρον, ἔμμηνά τε προτρέπον. ἀλλὰ καὶ καταπλασσόμενον ῥύπτει τε καὶ διαφορεῖ. καὶ θείη ἄν τις αὐτὸ κατὰ μὲν τὴν θερμότητα τῆς δευτέρας που τάξεως ἤδη συμπληρουμένης, κατὰ δὲ τὴν ξηρότητα τῆς τρίτης, ἤτοι μεσούσης καὶ συμπληρουμένης. τῷ χυλῷ δ’ αὐτοῦ καὶ πρὸς ὀξυδερκίαν μὲν χρῶνται μετὰ μέλιτος, οὐ μὴν ἀλλὰ καὶ διὰ ῥινῶν ἰκτέρους καθαίρουσι, καὶ πρὸς ὤτων ὀδύνας ἤδη κεχρονισμένας αὐτὸ παραλαμβάνουσι, ἐφ’ ὅσον ἐκφράξαι καὶ διακαθῆραι τόν τε πόρον αὐτὸν καὶ τὰς τῶν μηνίγγων ἀποφύσεις ἐστὶ χρεία.

8.16.34 [34. On polypody.] Polypody has a quality that is at once sweet and harsh predominating, so that it is of a drying power that is sufficiently non-biting. 8.16.35 [35. On horehound.] Horehound, just as it is bitter to the taste, so also for those who use it has an activity that agrees with such a flavour, unblocking both the liver and the spleen, and thoroughly cleansing the parts about the chest and the lung, and provoking the menses. But also, when applied as a plaster, it both cleanses and disperses. And one might place it, in respect of heat, somewhere in the second rank already being completed, and in respect of dryness, in the third, either at its middle or being completed. Its juice they also use, with honey, for sharpness of sight; and moreover they also purge jaundice through the nostrils with it, and they also take it up for pains of the ears that have already become chronic, in so far as there is need to unblock and thoroughly cleanse both the passage itself and the outgrowths of the meninges.

8.16.34 [34. On polypody.] Polypody has the sweet and at the same time harsh quality predominating, so that it is of a drying power that is quite free from biting. 8.16.35 [35. Περὶ πτελέας.] Πτελέας τοῖς φύλλοις ἐκολλήσαμέν ποτε πρόσφατον τραῦμα, θαῤῥήσαντες τῷ στυπτικήν τε ἅμα καὶ ῥυπτικὴν δύναμιν ἐμφέρεσθαι αὐτοῖς. ὁ δὲ φλοιὸς ἔτι καὶ μᾶλλον ὑπόπικρός τέ ἐστι καὶ στυπτικὸς, ὥστε καὶ λέπραν ἰᾶται σὺν ὄξει καὶ χλωρὸς δὲ ὢν ἔτι καὶ πρόσφατος, ἐὰν ὡς ἐπίδεσμος περιελιχθῇ τοῖς τραύμασιν, κολλᾷν αὐτὰ δύναται. καὶ αἱ ῥίζαι δὲ τῆς αὐτῆς εἰσι δυνάμεως, ὥστε καὶ τῷ ἀφεψήματι καταντλοῦσίν τινες, ὅσα πωρώσεως δεῖται κατάγματα.

8.16.38 [38. On elm.] With the leaves of elm we once glued together a fresh wound, trusting in the fact that an astringent and at the same time cleansing power is contained in them. The bark is still more slightly bitter and astringent, so that it even cures lepra with vinegar; and while it is still green and fresh, if it is wound around wounds like a bandage, it is able to glue them together. The roots too are of the same power, so that some also foment with the decoction those fractures which require the formation of a callus.

8.16.38 [38. On elm.] With the leaves of the elm we once agglutinated a fresh wound, confident in the fact that they carry an astringent and at the same time cleansing power. The bark is even more both somewhat bitter and astringent, so that with vinegar it even heals lepra; and while it is still green and fresh, if it is wound around wounds as a bandage, it is able to agglutinate them. The roots too are of the same power, so that some people also bathe with the decoction those fractures that require the formation of callus.

8.16.39 [λθ΄. Περὶ πτέριδος καὶ θηλυπτερίδος.] Πτέρις ῥίζαν μάλιστ’ ἔχει χρήσιμον. ἀναιρεῖ γὰρ ἕλμινθα πλατεῖαν, εἰ τεσσάρων δραχμῶν ὁλκήν τις αὐτῆς ἐν μελικράτῳ λάβοι. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον ἔμβρυα τὰ μὲν ζῶντα διαφθείρειν, τὰ νεκρὰ δ’ ἐκβάλλειν οὐδὲν θαυμαστόν. ἔστι γὰρ πικρὰ βραχύ τι στύψεως μετέχουσα. διὸ καὶ κατὰ τῶν ἑλκῶν ἐπιτιθεμένη δυνάμεώς ἐστιν ἰσχυρῶς ξηραντικῆς, οὐ μέντοι δακνώδης. ὁμοίαν δ’ αὐτῇ καὶ ἡ θηλυπτερὶς ὀνομαζομένη τὴν δύναμιν ἔχει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.16.39 [39. On pteris (male fern) and thelypteris (female fern).] Pteris has a root that is especially useful. For it destroys the broad worm, if one takes a weight of four drachms of it in honey-water (melikraton). And in the same way it is nothing to be wondered at that it destroys living embryos and expels dead ones. For it is bitter, partaking of a small degree of astringency. On this account, too, when applied to ulcers it is of a strongly drying power, yet not biting. And the so-called thelypteris has a power similar to it.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
8.16.39 [39. On fern and female fern.] Fern has a root that is especially useful. For it kills the broad worm, if one takes a weight of four drachms of it in honey-water. And in the same way it is no wonder that it destroys embryos that are living and expels those that are dead. For it is bitter, partaking of a little astringency. For this reason, when it is applied to ulcers too, it is of a strongly drying power, yet not biting. And the so-called female fern also has a power similar to it.

Περὶ πυκνοκόμου.] Πυκνοκόμου καὶ ἡ ῥίζα καὶ ὁ καρπὸς καὶ τὰ φύλλα διαφορητικῆς ἐστι καὶ ἑλκτικῆς δυνάμεως, ὄντα καὶ πρὸς τὴν γεῦσιν δριμέα. τὰ μὲν οὖν φύλλα φύματά τε καὶ δοθιῆνας διαφορεῖ, ὁ δὲ καρπὸς ἰσχυρότερος ὑπάρχει, καὶ ταῦτα μὲν ἐργάζεσθαι πέφυκε, μιγνύμενός τινι τῶν διαφορητικῶν καταπλάσματι, οἷόν πέρ ἐστι καὶ τὸ διὰ τοῦ κριθίνου· καὶ σκόλοπας δὲ καὶ ὅσα τοιαῦτα πρὸς τὴν ἐκτὸς ἐπιφάνειαν ἐπισπᾶται. ἡ δὲ ῥίζα πρὸς τὰ μὲν προειρημένα χείρων ἐστὶ καὶ ἀσθενεστέρα, χολῆς μέντοι τῆς ξανθῆς καθαρτική.

8.16.40 [40. On pyknokomon.] Of pyknokomon the root, the fruit and the leaves are all of a dispersing and drawing power, being also pungent to the taste. The leaves, then, disperse phymata and boils, while the fruit is stronger, and it is naturally able to accomplish these things when mixed with some one of the dispersing poultices, such as is also the one made with barley [meal]; and it also draws splinters and all such things out towards the outer surface. The root, however, is inferior and weaker for the purposes mentioned before, but it is purgative of yellow bile.

8.16.42 [42. On wheat.] Wheat, of what sort of nature it is as food, does not belong to the present treatise; but as a drug applied externally it is of the first order of heating things, yet it is not by nature either drying or moistening in any evident way. It also has something viscous and obstructive. The starch prepared from it becomes colder and more drying than wheat itself. And the plaster made from bread is of a more dispersing power than that made from grains of wheat, inasmuch as the bread has previously received both salt and leaven; for leaven is of a power that attracts and disperses what lies in the depth.

Περὶ ῥήου.] Ῥῆον, ἔνιοι δὲ προσαγορεύουσιν αὐτὸ ῥᾶ, μικτῆς ἐστι κράσεώς τε καὶ δυνάμεως. ἔχει μὲν γάρ τι καὶ γεῶδες ψυχρὸν, ὡς ἡ στύψις δηλοῦ σύνεστι δέ τις αὐτῷ καὶ θερμότης, ὑπόδριμυ γὰρ ἐπιπλέον διαμασώμενον φαίνεται. καὶ μὴν ὡς ἀερώδους τινὸς οὐσίας μετέχει λεπτομεροῦς δηλοῖ μὲν καὶ ἡ χαυνότης τε καὶ ἡ κουφότης, ἀτὰρ οὐχ ἥκιστα καὶ τὰ ἔργα. δι’ αὐτὸ γάρ τοι τοῦτο καί τοι στῦφον ὅμως οὐ σπάσματα μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ῥήγματα καὶ ὀρθόπνοιαν ὠφελεῖ. οὕτω δὴ καὶ πελιώματα καὶ λειχῆνας ἰᾶται μετ’ ὄξους ἐπαλειφόμενον. ὅτι δὲ καὶ τὰ τῆς στύψεως ἔργα γενναῖα, καταμαθεῖν ἔστιν ἐξ ὧν ὀνίνησιν αἱμοπτυϊκοὺς καὶ κοιλιακοὺς καὶ δυσεντερικούς. οὐ γὰρ ἀντιπράττει τῷ γεώδει ψυχρῷ τὸ λεπτομερὲς ἀερῶδες. ἀλλ’ οὖν τὸ ποδηγοῦν τε καὶ πρὸς βάθος ἄγον αἴτιον ἰσχυροτέρας ἐνεργείας γίνεται.

8.17.3 [3. On rheon.] Rheon — some call it rha — is of a mixed mixture and power. For it has something earthy and cold, as its astringency shows; but there is also present in it a certain heat, for when chewed for a longer time it appears somewhat pungent. And indeed, that it partakes of a certain airy, fine-particled substance is shown both by its looseness and its lightness, and not least also by its effects. For on this very account, although it is astringent, it nonetheless benefits not only strains but also ruptures and orthopnoea. So too it cures livid marks and lichens when smeared on with vinegar. And that the effects of its astringency too are vigorous one may learn from the fact that it helps those who spit blood, coeliacs and dysenterics. For the fine-particled airy part does not work against the earthy cold; rather, by guiding it and leading it into the depth, it becomes the cause of a stronger activity.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καί τοι (read as καίτοι), σπάσματα, πελιώματα, ποδηγοῦν, ἐπιπλέον, χαυνότης
8.17.3 [3. On rhēon.] Rhēon, which some call rha, is of a mixed mixture and power. For it has something earthy and cold, as its astringency shows; but a certain heat is also present in it, for when chewed for a longer time it appears somewhat pungent. And indeed, that it partakes of a fine-particled, airy substance is shown both by its loose texture and its lightness, but not least also by its effects. For precisely because of this, although it is astringent, it nevertheless benefits not only strains (spasmata) but also ruptures and orthopnoea. So too it heals livid marks and leichenes when smeared on with vinegar. That the effects of its astringency are also vigorous may be learned from the benefit it gives to those who spit blood, to coeliacs and to dysenterics. For the fine-particled airy part does not work against the earthy cold part; rather, what guides it on its way and leads it into the depth becomes the cause of a stronger action.

8.17.4 [δ΄. Περὶ ῥητινῶν.] Ῥητίναι πᾶσαι ξηραίνουσι καὶ θερμαίνουσι. διαφέρουσι δ’ ἀλλήλων τῷ τε πλέον ἢ ἔλαττον ἔχειν ἔν τε τῇ γεύσει δριμὺ κᾀν τῇ δυνάμει θερμαῖνον καὶ τῷ τὰς μὲν ἧττον αὐτῶν εἶναι λεπτομερεῖς, τὰς δὲ μᾶλλον, καὶ τῷ τὰς μὲν στύψεως μετέχειν, τὰς δ’ οὔ. προκέκριται δ’ εἰκότως ἐξ ἁπασῶν ἡ σχινίνη, μαστίχην δ’ αὐτὴν ὀνομάζουσι. πρὸς γὰρ τῷ στύψεως ὀλίγης μετέχειν, ὡς καὶ ταῖς κατὰ τὸν στόμαχον καὶ τὴν γαστέρα καὶ τὸ ἧπαρ ἀτονίαις καὶ φλεγμοναῖς ἁρμόττειν, ἔτι καὶ τὸ ξηραίνειν καὶ ἀλύπως αὐτῇ πάρεστιν. ἥκιστα γάρ ἐστι δριμεῖα καὶ μάλιστα λεπτομερής. τῶν δ’ ἄλλων ἡ τερμινθίνη πρωτεύει, σαφῆ μὲν οὐχ ὁμοίως τῇ μαστίχῃ τὴν στύψιν ἔχουσα, πικρότητα δέ τινα κεκτημένη, δι’ ἣν καὶ μᾶλλον τῆς μαστίχης διαφορεῖ. διὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν ταύτην ποιότητα καὶ τὸ ῥύπτειν αὐτῇ πάρεστιν ἐπὶ τοσοῦτον, ὥστε καὶ ψώρας ἰᾶσθαι. καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ τὰ ἐκ βάθους ἕλκει μᾶλλον τῶν ἄλλων ῥητινῶν, ὅτι καὶ λεπτομερὴς ἐκείνων μᾶλλόν ἐστι. ἥ γέ τοι πευκίνη, καὶ ταύτης ἔτι μᾶλλον ἡ στροβιλίνη, δριμύτεραι μὲν αὐτῆς εἰσιν, οὐ μὴν οὔτε διαφοροῦσι μᾶλλον οὔτ’ ἐπισπῶνται. μέσαι δ’ αὐτῶν εἰσιν ἡ πιτυΐνη καὶ ἐλατίνη, δριμύτεραι μὲν οὖσαι τῆς τερμινθίνης, ἧττον δὲ δριμεῖαι τῆς πευκίνης τε καὶ στροβιλίνης. ἔχει δὲ καὶ μαλακτικὸν ἡ τερμινθίνη ῥητίνη. δευτέρα δ’ ἐστὶν ἐν τῷ μαλάσσειν ἡ σχινίνη, καθάπερ ἡ κυπαρισσίνη δριμεῖα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καθάπερ ἡ κυπαρισσίνη δριμεῖα, στόμαχον
8.17.4 [4. On resins.] All resins dry and heat. They differ from one another in having more or less of what is pungent in taste and heating in power, and in that some of them are less fine-particled and others more so, and in that some partake of astringency and others do not. Reasonably, the one from the lentisk has been preferred out of all of them; they call it mastic. For in addition to its partaking of a little astringency, so that it is suited to the weaknesses (atonies) and inflammations about the stomach (stomachos), the belly and the liver, it also possesses the power of drying, and that without distress. For it is least pungent and most fine-particled. Of the others the terebinth resin holds first place, not having its astringency as manifest as mastic does, but possessing a certain bitterness, on account of which it is also more dispersing than mastic. Through this same quality it also possesses a cleansing power, to such a degree that it even cures psora (scabby itch). And indeed it also draws up things from the depth more than the other resins, because it is more fine-particled than they. The pine resin (peukine), and still more than this the stone-pine resin (strobiline), are more pungent than it, yet they neither disperse more nor draw more. Intermediate among them are the pitys-pine resin and the fir resin, being more pungent than the terebinth resin, but less pungent than the pine and stone-pine resins. The terebinth resin also has a softening power. Second in softening is the lentisk resin, just as the cypress resin is pungent.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πευκίνη, στροβιλίνη, πιτυΐνη, ψώρας, καθάπερ ἡ κυπαρισσίνη δριμεῖα, ἀλύπως
8.17.4 [4. On resins.] All resins dry and heat. They differ from one another in having more or less of what is pungent in taste and heating in power; in some of them being less fine-particled and others more; and in some partaking of astringency and others not. Of them all, the resin of the schinos is with good reason given first place; they call it mastic. For besides partaking of a little astringency, so that it suits atonies and inflammations of the stomach, the belly and the liver, it also has the property of drying without causing distress. For it is the least pungent and the most fine-particled. Of the others, the terebinth resin holds first place. It does not have an astringency as evident as that of mastic, but it possesses a certain bitterness, through which it also disperses more than mastic does. Through this same quality it also has a cleansing property to such a degree that it even heals psōrai. Moreover it draws out what lies in the depth more than the other resins do, because it is also more fine-particled than they are. The peukē resin, and still more than this the strobilos resin, are more pungent than it, yet they neither disperse more nor draw more. Intermediate between them are the pitys resin and the fir resin, being more pungent than the terebinth resin but less pungent than the peukē and strobilos resins. The terebinth resin also has something softening. Second in softening is the schinos resin, just as the cypress resin is pungent.

8.17.8 [8. On pomegranates.] Every pomegranate partakes of the astringent quality, yet it does not predominate in all of them. For some of them are sour, and some are sweet more than harsh. And indeed the benefits of each of them must necessarily come about in accordance with the predominating quality. It has been stated in the fourth of these treatises concerning the sweet, the harsh and the sour flavour, so that from those discussions it is possible for you to reason out also the difference among pomegranates. The seeds are more astringent and more drying than the juice; and still more so than these are the rinds, which are called sidia. Of a similar power to these are the kytinoi (pomegranate flowers).

8.17.9 [θ΄. Περὶ ῥοός.] Ῥοῦς τὸ θαμνῶδες Φυτὸν στύφει καὶ ξηραίνει, ὥσπερ καὶ οἱ βυρσοδέψαι πρὸς τὸ ξηρᾶναι καὶ στῦψαι τὰ δέρματα χρῶνται τῷ φυτῷ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ὀνομάζεται βυρσοδεψικὴ τοῖς ἰατροῖς. ὅ τε καρπὸς αὐτῆς μάλιστα καὶ ὁ χυλὸς εἰς χρείαν ἥκουσιν, ἱκανῶς αὐστηρᾶς ὄντες ποιότητος. ὁμολογεῖ δὲ καὶ ἡ κατὰ μέρος ἅπασα χρῆσις τῇ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν αἰσθήσει. εἴη ἂν οὖν καὶ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ξηραντικὸν μὲν ἐκ τῆς τρίτης τάξεως, ψυκτικὸν δὲ ἐκ τῆς δευτέρας.

8.17.9 [9. On rhous (sumach).] Rhous, the shrub-like plant, is astringent and drying, just as the tanners too use the plant for drying and making the hides astringent, and for this reason it is called by the physicians the tanners' plant (byrsodepsike). It is chiefly its fruit and its juice that come into use, being of a sufficiently harsh quality. And the whole of its use in particular cases agrees with the perception by taste. This drug too, then, would be drying in the third rank, and cooling in the second.

8.17.9 [9. Its fruit above all, and its juice, come into use, being of a considerably harsh quality. And all its use in particular cases agrees with the perception by taste. 8.17.10 [ι΄. Περὶ ῥύπου.] Ῥύπος ὁ μὲν ἀπὸ τῶν ἐν τοῖς γυμνασίοις ἀνδριάντων, ἐν οἷς ἂν ἄφθονον ἔλαιον κεῖται, διαφορητικός τέ ἐστι καὶ μαλακτικός. ὁ δ’ ἐν ταῖς παλαίστραις ἐφ’ ὅσον τῆς κόνεως μετείληφε, ἐπὶ τοσοῦτον διενήνοχεν. ὀνομάζουσι δὲ τοῦτον τὸν ῥύπον πάτον. ὁ μὲν δὴ πρότερος ἀπέπτων φυμάτων ἐστὶ διαφορητικὸς, ὁ δὲ δεύτερος ἄριστον ἴαμα φλεγμονῆς τιτθῶν ἐστι. καὶ γὰρ τὸ πυρῶδες αὐτῶν σβέννυσι καὶ τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον ἀναστέλλει καὶ τὸ περιεχόμενον διαφορεῖ· σύγκειται γὰρ ἐκ κόνεως καὶ ἐλαίου καὶ ἀνθρωπείου ῥύπου καὶ ἱδρῶτος. ἡ μὲν οὖν κόνις ἐμπλα στικόν τέ ἐστι καὶ ψυκτικὸν καὶ ἀποκρουστικὸν, τὸ δ’ ἔλαιον μαλακτικὸν, ὁ δ’ ἱδρὼς καὶ ὁ ῥύπος διαφορητικά. ὁ δ’ ἀπὸ τῶν ἀνδριάντων οὔτε τὴν κόνιν ἔχων καὶ τοῦ χαλκοῦ τινα προσειληφὼς ἰὸν εἰκότως δριμύτερός ἐστιν. ἐμνημονεύσαμεν δὲ τούτου καὶ νῦν, ὅτι τὸ πλεῖστον ἐν αὐτῷ τῆς ἐλαιώδους οὐσίας ἐστὶν, ἐκ φυτοῦ τὴν γένεσιν ἐχούσης. ἀναγκαῖον δ’ ἔσται κᾀν τοῖς ἀπὸ ζώων αὐτοῦ μνημονεῦσαι διὰ τὸν ἱδρῶτα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πάτον
8.17.10 [10. On dirt.] Dirt: that from the statues in the gymnasia, on which abundant oil lies, is both dispersing and softening. That in the wrestling-schools differs from it to the extent that it has taken up a share of the dust. They call this dirt patos. The former, then, is dispersing of unconcocted swellings, while the second is the best remedy for inflammation of the breasts; for it both quenches their fiery quality and represses the influx and disperses what is contained in them. For it is composed of dust and oil and human dirt and sweat. The dust, then, is emplastic and cooling and repelling, the oil softening, and the sweat and the dirt dispersing. But that from the statues, since it does not have the dust and has taken on some verdigris from the bronze, is reasonably more pungent. We have mentioned it here too, because the greatest part of it consists of the oil-like substance, which has its origin from a plant. But it will be necessary to mention it also among the things derived from animals, on account of the sweat.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἂν ... κεῖται (indicative with ἄν; one expects κέηται), οὔτε τὴν κόνιν ἔχων καὶ (οὔτε without a matching οὔτε; taken as 'having no dust and'), ἐμπλαστικόν (rendered 'emplastic', i.e. clogging/obstructing), πάτον (left as the term 'patos'), φυμάτων (left as 'phymata')
8.17.10 [10. On rhypos.] The rhypos from the statues in the gymnasia, on which abundant oil lies, is both dispersing and softening. That in the palaestrae differs from it to the extent that it has taken up dust; this rhypos they call patos. The former, then, is dispersing of unconcocted phymata, while the second is an excellent remedy for phlegmone of the breasts. For it quenches their fiery heat, checks what flows in, and disperses what is contained there; for it is composed of dust, oil, human rhypos and sweat. Now the dust is emplastic, cooling and repelling; the oil is softening; and the sweat and the rhypos are dispersing. The rhypos from the statues, since it has no dust and has also taken up some verdigris (ios) from the bronze, is reasonably more pungent. We have mentioned it here too because the greater part of it consists of oil-like substance, which has its origin from a plant. But it will be necessary to mention it also among the drugs from animals, on account of the sweat.

8.18.1 [α΄. Περὶ σαγαπηνοῦ.] Σαγαπηνὸν ὀπός ἐστι θερμὸς καὶ λεπτομερὴς, ὡσαύτως τοῖς ἄλλοις ὀποῖς. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ ῥυπτικὸν, ᾧ καὶ τὰς ἐν ὀφθαλμοῖς οὐλὰς ἀποκαθαίρει τε καὶ λεπτύνει. οὐ μὴν ἀλλὰ καὶ ὑποχύσεσι καὶ ἀμβλυωπίαις ταῖς διὰ πάχος ὑγρῶν γινομέναις ἀγαθόν ἐστι φάρμακον. αὐτὸ δὲ τὸ φυτὸν ἐξ οὗ γίνεται νάρθηκι παραπλήσιον ὑπάρχον ἀσθενές τε καὶ ἄχρηστόν ἐστιν, ὀνομάζεται δὲ δηλονότι πρότερον ἐκεῖνο σαγαπηνόν. ὁ γὰρ ὀπὸς αὐτοῦ καταχρωμένοις οὕτω προσαγορεύεται, καθάπερ καὶ ἄλλα πολλὰ δι’ ἐλλιποῦς λέξεως ὀνομάζομεν ἐκ καταχρήσεως. τελεώτερον γὰρ ἦν λέγειν αὐτὸν οὐ σαγαπηνὸν, ἀλλ’ ὀπὸν σαγαπηνοῦ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑποχύσεσι
8.18.1 [1. On sagapenon.] Sagapenon is a juice, hot and fine-particled, in the same way as the other juices. It also has something cleansing, by which it both cleans away and thins the scars in the eyes. Moreover, it is also a good drug for suffusions and for dimnesses of sight that arise through thickness of the humours. The plant itself from which it comes, being similar to the narthex, is weak and useless; and clearly it is that which is called sagapenon in the first place. For its juice is so termed by those who use the word loosely, just as we also name many other things by catachresis through an elliptical expression. For it would have been more complete to call it not sagapenon but juice of sagapenon.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑποχύσεσι, ἀσθενές, καταχρωμένοις, ἐκ καταχρήσεως
8.18.1 [1. On sagapenum.] Sagapenum is a sap, hot and fine-particled, like the other saps. It also has something cleansing, by which it both cleans away and thins the scars in the eyes. Moreover, it is also a good drug for suffusions and for amblyopias that arise through thickness of the fluids. The plant itself from which it comes, which resembles giant fennel, is weak and useless; and it is evidently that plant which is called sagapenum in the first instance. For its sap is so named by those who use the word loosely, just as we also name many other things, by an abuse of terms, with an elliptical expression. For it would have been more complete to call it not 'sagapenum' but 'sap of sagapenum'.

8.18.7 [7. On seris.] Seris is a somewhat bitter vegetable, and the wild kind more so; and for this very reason some call it pikris, while others name it kichorion. Both share in the astringent quality, just as chondrile does too; for this also is a species of seris.

8.18.8 [8. On seriphon.] Seriphon. This has been discussed in the sixth book, in the account of apsinthion. For it is similar to apsinthion in appearance and taste, yet it nonetheless differs: in taste, in that it is not astringent in the same way as that one, and heats and is bitter to a greater degree, with a certain saltiness; and in power, in that it is bad for the stomach and kills worms more than apsinthion does, both when applied externally and when taken into the body. And indeed one must place it in the second order, at its intense degree, as regards heating, and in the third as regards drying.

8.18.13 [13. On sideritis.] Sideritis has indeed also something cleansing, but the greater part of it is moist and moderately cold, and it has partaken of some slight astringency, whence it is both non-inflammatory and agglutinating. Περὶ σικύου ἐδωδίμου.] Σίκυος ἐδώδιμος ὁ μὲν ἤδη πέπων λεπτομερεστέρας ἐστὶν οὐσίας, ὁ δὲ μὴ τοιοῦτος παχυμερεστέρας. οὐ μὴν ἀλλὰ καὶ ῥυπτικῆς τε καὶ τμητικῆς μετειλήφασι δυνάμεως, ὅθεν οὐρητικοί τ’ εἰσὶ καὶ λαμπρύνουσι τὸ σῶμα, καὶ μᾶλλον εἰ ξηράνας τις τὸ σπέρμα, κᾄπειτα κόψας τε καὶ σήσας ῥύμματι χρῷτο. κρατεῖ δ’ ἐν αὐτοῖς ὑγρὰ καὶ ψυχρὰ κρᾶσις, οὐ μὴν ἰσχυρῶς, ἀλλ’ ὡς ἐν τῇ δευτέρᾳ που τάξει τίθεται. εἰ μέντοι τις τὸ σπέρμα ξηραίνειεν ἢ τὴν ῥίζαν, οὐκέτ’ οὐδὲ τῆς ὑγρᾶς ἐστι φύσεως, ἀλλ’ ἤδη τῆς ξηραινούσης κατὰ τὴν πρώτην που τάξιν, ἢ καὶ τὴν δευτέραν ἀρχομένην. ἔστι δὲ καὶ τὸ ῥυπτικὸν ἐν τούτοις πλέον ἢ ἐν τῇ σαρκὶ τοῦ καρποῦ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.18.14 [14. On the edible cucumber.] The edible cucumber, when already ripe, is of a more fine-particled substance, but when not such, of a more coarse-particled one. Nonetheless they also partake of a cleansing and cutting power, whence they are diuretic and brighten the body, and more so if one dries the seed and then, having pounded and sifted it, uses it as a cleansing wash. A moist and cold mixture prevails in them, yet not strongly, but such that it is placed somewhere in the second rank. If, however, one were to dry the seed or the root, it is no longer even of the moist nature, but already of the drying nature in about the first rank, or even the beginning of the second. The cleansing quality, too, is greater in these than in the flesh of the fruit.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῥύμματι, λαμπρύνουσι
8.18.14 [14. On the edible cucumber.] Of the edible cucumber, the one that is already ripe is of a more fine-particled substance, while the one that is not so is of a more coarse-particled substance. Nevertheless, they also have a share in a cleansing and cutting power, whence they are both diuretic and make the body bright, and more so if one dries the seed, and then pounds and sifts it and uses it as a detergent. In them a moist and cold mixture prevails, not strongly, however, but such that it is placed somewhere in the second order. If, however, one dries the seed or the root, it is no longer of a moist nature either, but already of a drying one, somewhere in the first order, or even at the beginning of the second. And the cleansing quality is also greater in these than in the flesh of the fruit.

8.18.15 [ιε΄. Περὶ σικύου ἀγρίου.] Σικύου ἀγρίου καὶ ὁ τοῦ καρποῦ χυλὸς, ὃν ἐλατήριον ὀνομάζουσιν, οὐχ ἥκιστα δὲ καὶ ὁ τῆς ῥίζης τε καὶ τῶν φύλλων, ἐπιτήδειοι τυγχάνουσιν ὄντες εἰς τὰς ἰάσεις. τὸ μὲν οὖν ἐλατήριον ἔμμηνα προκαλεῖται καὶ τὰ κυούμενα διαφθείρει προστιθέμενον, ὥσπερ καὶ τὰ ἄλλα τὰ πικρά τε ἅμα καὶ λεπτομερῆ, μάλιστα εἰ καὶ θερμότητός τινος μετέχει, καθάπερ οὖν καὶ τὸ ἐλατήριον. ἄκρως μὲν γάρ ἐστι πικρὸν, ἐπ’ ὀλίγον δὲ θερμὸν, ὡς ἐκ τῆς δευτέρας τάξεως εἶναι κατὰ θερμότητα. εὐθὺς δὲ τὸ τοιοῦτον καὶ διαφορητικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως· οὕτω γοῦν αὐτῷ καὶ συναγχικοὺς διαχρίουσιν ἔνιοι μετὰ μέλιτος, ἢ ἐλαίου παλαιοῦ. ἀγαθὸν δὲ καὶ τοῖς ἰκτερικοῖς ἐγχεόμενον μετὰ γάλακτος ταῖς ῥισίν. οὕτω δὲ χρώμενον καὶ κεφαλαλγίας ἰᾶται. ﻿ τὸ μὲν οὖν ἐλατήριον τοιοῦτον, ὁ δὲ τῆς ῥίζης χυλὸς, ὥσπερ γε καὶ ὁ τῶν φύλλων, ὅμοιοι μὲν ἐλατηρίῳ τὴν δύναμιν, ἀσθενέστεροι δέ. καὶ αὐτὴ δὲ ἡ ῥίζα παραπλησίας ἐστὶ δυνάμεως. καὶ γὰρ ῥύπτει καὶ διαφορεῖ καὶ μαλάττει, ξηραντικώτερος δ’ αὐτῆς ἐστιν ὁ φλοιός.

8.18.15 [15. On wild cucumber.] Of the wild cucumber both the juice of the fruit, which they call elaterion, and no less also that of the root and of the leaves, happen to be suitable for cures. Elaterion, then, provokes the menses and destroys the foetus when applied as a pessary, just as do the other things that are at once bitter and fine-particled, especially if they also partake of some heat, as indeed elaterion does. For it is bitter in the highest degree, but hot to a small extent, so as to be of the second order in respect of heat. Such a thing is also at once of dispersing power; thus, at any rate, some anoint with it those suffering from synanche, together with honey or old oil. It is good also for those suffering from jaundice when poured into the nostrils with milk; and used in this way it also cures headaches. Such, then, is elaterion; but the juice of the root, as also that of the leaves, is similar to elaterion in power, though weaker. And the root itself is of a closely similar power; for it cleanses and disperses and softens, but its bark is more drying than it.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προστιθέμενον
8.18.15 [15. On the wild cucumber.] Of the wild cucumber, both the juice of the fruit, which they call elaterium, and, not least, that of the root and of the leaves, are suitable for treatments. Elaterium, then, provokes the menses and destroys what is being carried in the womb when applied as a pessary, as do the other substances that are at once bitter and fine-particled, especially if they also partake of some heat, as indeed elaterium does. For it is extremely bitter, but hot only to a slight degree, so as to belong to the second order in respect of heat. And such a substance is at once also of dispersing power; thus some anoint those suffering from synanche with it, together with honey or old oil. It is good also for those with jaundice when poured into the nostrils with milk. And used in this way it also cures headaches. Such, then, is elaterium. The juice of the root, as also that of the leaves, is similar to elaterium in power, but weaker. And the root itself is also of a closely similar power. For it cleanses, disperses and softens; but its bark is more drying than it.

8.18.16 [16. On silphium.] Of silphium the juice is the hottest, but nonetheless the leaves too, and the stalk and the root, heat vigorously. All of them are of a rather flatulent substance and on that account hard to digest. When applied externally, however, upon the body they are more active, and most of all the juice, which possesses a considerable drawing power. And indeed it also has something reducing and melting-away in it, on account of its aforesaid mixture.

8.18.16 [16. On silphium.] Of silphium the sap is the hottest, but nevertheless the leaves, the stalk and the root also heat vigorously. Applied externally to the body, though, they are more efficacious, and the sap most of all, since it is of a considerably drawing power. 8.18.25 [25. On skordion.] Skordion. This is compounded of manifold humours and powers. 8.18.26 [κστ΄. Περὶ σκορόδου.] Σκόροδον ξηραίνει καὶ θερμαίνει κατὰ τὴν τετάρτην ἀπόστασιν. τὸ δ’ ὀφιοσκόροδον ὀνομαζόμενον ἄγριόν ἐστι σκόροδον ἰσχυρότερον τοῦ ἡμέρου, ὥσπερ καὶ τὰ ἄλλα τὰ ἄγρια.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.18.26 [26. On garlic.] Garlic dries and heats in the fourth degree of departure. What is called ophioscorodon (snake-garlic) is a wild garlic, stronger than the cultivated kind, just as the other wild plants are.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
8.18.26 [26. On garlic.] Garlic dries and heats in the fourth degree. The so-called ophioskorodon ('snake-garlic') is a wild garlic, stronger than the cultivated, just as the other wild plants are too.

Simp.8.18.30Simp. 8.18.30, ch. 8.18 difficulty high (7.21/100; thin 7, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 14/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
8.18.30 [λ΄. Περὶ σμύρνης.] Σμύρνα τῆς δευτέρας ἐστὶ τάξεως τῶν θερμαινόντων τε καὶ ξηραινόντων. ἐπιπλαττομένη γοῦν τοῖς ἐν κεφαλῇ τραύμασιν κολλᾷν αὐτὰ δύναται. μετέχει δὲ καὶ πικρότητος οὐκ ὀλίγης, δι’ ἣν ἔμβρυα καὶ ἕλμινθας κτείνει τε καὶ ἐκβάλλει. ὑπάρχει δ’ αὐτῇ καὶ τὸ ῥυπτικὸν ἐντεῦθεν. οὕτω γοῦν καὶ ταῖς ὀφθαλμικαῖς μίγνυται δυνάμεσιν, ὅσαι πρὸς ἕλκη καὶ οὐλὰς παχείας συντίθενται. κατὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν αἰτίαν ἐπιμίγνυται τοῖς πρὸς βῆχα χρονίαν καὶ ἆσθμα διδομένοις φαρμάκοις, οὐ μὴν τραχύνει γε τὴν ἀρτηρίαν ὡς ἔνια τῶν ῥυπτόντων, ἀλλ’ οὕτως ἔχει μετρίαν τὴν ῥύψιν, ὥστ’ ἔνιοι καὶ τοῖς ἀρτηριακοῖς ὀνομαζομένοις φαρμάκοις μιγνύουσιν αὐτὴν ὡς θερμαῖνόν τε καὶ ξηραῖνον αὐτάρκως φάρμακον, οὐδὲν εὐλαβούμενοι· τὴν ἐκ τῆς πικρότητος ῥύψιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δυνάμεσιν
8.18.30 [30. On myrrh.] Myrrh belongs to the second order of things that heat and dry. At any rate, when plastered upon wounds in the head it is able to glue them together. It also partakes of no small bitterness, on account of which it both kills and expels embryos and worms. And from this source it also possesses the cleansing property. So at any rate it is also mixed into the ophthalmic preparations, those which are compounded for ulcers and thick scars. For the same reason it is also mixed into the drugs given for chronic cough and asthma; yet it does not roughen the windpipe as some of the cleansing agents do, but has its cleansing so moderate that some mix it even into the drugs called arteriacs, as a drug that heats and dries sufficiently, taking no precaution at all against the cleansing that comes from its bitterness.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: εὐλαβούμενοι· τὴν ἐκ τῆς πικρότητος ῥύψιν (the raised point before τὴν looks corrupt; I have taken τὴν ... ῥύψιν as the object of εὐλαβούμενοι), δυνάμεσιν (taken as 'preparations', i.e. compound remedies), ἀρτηριακοῖς (drugs for the windpipe; rendered 'arteriac'), κολλᾷν (rendered 'conglutinate')
8.18.30 [30. On myrrh.] Myrrh belongs to the second order of things that heat and dry. At any rate, when applied as a plaster to wounds in the head, it is able to conglutinate them. It also partakes of no small bitterness, on account of which it both kills and expels embryos and worms. Its cleansing property also comes to it from this source. Thus, at any rate, it is also mixed into those ophthalmic preparations that are compounded for ulcers and thick scars. And for the same reason it is mixed into the drugs given for chronic cough and asthma. Yet it does not roughen the windpipe, as some of the cleansing drugs do; rather, its cleansing is so moderate that some even mix it into the drugs called arteriac, as a drug that heats and dries sufficiently, and they are not at all wary of the cleansing that comes from its bitterness.
Flags (places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors): none raised.
