Greek (Kühn)
8.18.31 [λα΄. Περὶ σμύρνης βοϊκῆς.] Σμύρνα βοϊκὴ δύναμιν ἔχει θερμαντικὴν, διαχυτικὴν, μαλακτικήν. 8.18.32 [λβ΄. Περὶ σμυρνίου.] Σμύρνιον, οἱ δ’ ἱπποσέλινον ὀνομάζουσιν ἄγριον, ἐκ ταὐτοῦ γένους ἐστὶ καὶ τοῦτο τῷ σελίνῳ τε καὶ πετροσελίνῳ. καὶ εἴη ἂν σελίνου μὲν ἰσχυρότερον, πετροσελίνου δὲ ἀσθενέστερον. ἐμμήνων τε οὖν ἀγωγόν ἐστι καὶ οὐρητικὸν καὶ θερμὸν καὶ ξηρὸν κατὰ τὴν τρίτην τάξιν. οἱ δ’ ἐν Κιλικίᾳ πετροσέλινον ὀνομάζουσιν ἐν τῷ Ἀμάνῳ γεννώμενον. ἔστι μέντοι καὶ αὐτὸ σμύρνιον, ἧττον δ’ ἐστὶν δριμὺ πετροσελίνου καὶ σμυρνίου, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ κατὰ τραυμάτων ἐπιτίθεσθαι δύναται τῷ ξηραίνειν ἀλύπως. ἀλλὰ καὶ διαφορητικόν ἐστι τῶν ἐσκληρυσμένων. ἡ δ’ ἄλλη δύναμις αὐτοῦ παραπλησία σελίνῳ τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ πετροσελίνῳ, διὸ καὶ τῷ σπέρματι χρώμεθα πρὸς τὰ καταμήνιά τε καὶ οὖρα καὶ ἄσθματα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βοϊκῆς, βοϊκὴ
8.18.31 [31. On bovine myrrh.] Bovine myrrh has a heating, diffusing, softening power. 8.18.32 [32. On smyrnion.] Smyrnion, which some call wild hipposelinon (horse-celery), is also of the same genus as celery and parsley. And it would be stronger than celery, but weaker than parsley. It is therefore a drawer-down of the menses and a diuretic, and hot and dry in the third order. Those in Cilicia give the name parsley (petroselinon) to that which grows on Mount Amanus. It too, however, is a smyrnion, but it is less pungent than parsley and smyrnion, and for this reason it can also be applied to wounds, since it dries without causing distress. But it is also dispersing of hardened parts. Its other power is similar to celery and parsley, wherefore we also use its seed for the menses and urine and asthmas.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: βοϊκῆς / βοϊκὴ (the sense of 'ox' myrrh is unclear and the word may be corrupt), διαχυτικὴν (rendered 'diffusing'), ἧττον δ’ ἐστὶν δριμὺ πετροσελίνου καὶ σμυρνίου (comparing a smyrnion with smyrnion is odd; the text may be corrupt), τῶν ἐσκληρυσμένων (rendered 'hardened swellings'; the Greek has only 'hardened [things]')
8.18.31 [31. On 'ox' myrrh.] 'Ox' myrrh has a heating, diffusing, softening power. 8.18.32 [32. On smyrnion.] Smyrnion, which some call wild horse-celery, is also of the same genus as celery and parsley. It would be stronger than celery but weaker than parsley. So it promotes the menses, is diuretic, and is hot and dry in the third order. The people in Cilicia give the name parsley to the plant that grows on Amanus. This too, however, is a smyrnion, but it is less pungent than parsley and smyrnion, and for this reason it can also be applied to wounds, because it dries without causing pain. It is also dispersing of hardened swellings. Its other power is similar to that of celery and parsley, and for this reason we also use its seed for the menses, urine and asthma.

Simp.8.18.33Simp. 8.18.33, ch. 8.18 difficulty low (1.56/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
8.18.33 [λγ΄. Περὶ σόγχου.] Σόγχος ἐπειδὰν μὲν τελειωθῇ, τῶν ἀκανθωδῶν ἐστιν φυτῶν, χλωρὸς δ’ ἔτι καὶ ἁπαλὸς ἐσθίεται τοῖς ἄλλοις ἀγρίοις λαχάνοις ὡσαύτως. ἡ κρᾶσις δ’ αὐτοῦ μικτή πώς ἐστι, σύγκειται γὰρ ἐξ ὑδατώδους τε καὶ γεώδους οὐσίας, ἀμφοῖν ἀτρέμα ψυχρῶν. καὶ γὰρ στύψεώς τι μετέχει καὶ σαφῶς ἐμψύχει, καταπλασσόμενός τε καὶ ἐσθιόμενος. ἐπειδὴ δὲ τελέως ξηρανθῇ, γεώδης ἡ κρᾶσις αὐτοῦ γίνεται μετρίως θερμότητα ἔχουσα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.18.33 [33. On sonchos.] Sonchos, when it has reached maturity, is one of the thorny plants, but while still green and tender it is eaten in the same way as the other wild vegetables. Its mixture is in a way mixed, for it is composed of a watery and an earthy substance, both of them slightly cold. For indeed it partakes of some astringency and manifestly cools, both when applied as a plaster and when eaten. But when it has been completely dried, its mixture becomes earthy, possessing a moderate degree of heat.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
8.18.33 [33. On sonchos.] Sonchos, once it has reached maturity, is one of the thorny plants, but while still green and tender it is eaten in the same way as the other wild vegetables. Its mixture is in a certain way mixed, for it is composed of a watery and an earthy substance, both of them mildly cold. For it partakes of some astringency and clearly cools, whether applied as a poultice or eaten. But when it has been completely dried, its mixture becomes earthy, possessing a moderate heat.

8.18.36 [λστ΄. Περὶ σταφυλίνου.] Σταφυλῖνος ὁ μὲν ἥμερος ἀσθενέστερος, ὁ δ’ ἄγριος ἰσχυρότερος εἰς ἅπαντα. οὖρα δὲ κινεῖ καὶ καταμήνια προτρέπει καὶ σύμπασα μὲν ἡ πόα, μάλιστα δὲ τὸ σπέρμα καὶ ἡ ῥίζα. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ ῥυπτικὸν ἐν ἑαυτῷ, διὸ καὶ τὰ φαγεδαινικὰ τῶν ἑλκῶν ἔνιοι τοῖς φύλλοις αὐτοῦ χλωροῖς μετὰ μέλιτος καταπλάττουσι ὑπὲρ τοῦ καθαρὰ ποιῆσαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.18.36 [36. On staphylinos.] Staphylinos: the cultivated kind is weaker, the wild kind stronger in every respect. It moves urine and promotes the menses—the whole herb does so, but especially the seed and the root. It also has something cleansing in it, which is why some people apply its fresh leaves with honey as a plaster to phagedaenic ulcers in order to make them clean.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σταφυλίνου
8.18.36 [36. On staphylinos.] Of staphylinos, the cultivated kind is weaker and the wild kind stronger in all respects. It moves urine and promotes the menses; the whole herb does this, but above all the seed and the root. It also has something cleansing in it, and for this reason some people plaster phagedaenic ulcers with its leaves, while green, together with honey, in order to make them clean.

Greek (Kühn)
8.18.38 [λη΄. Περὶ στοιβῆς.] Στοιβῆς ὁ καρπὸς καὶ τὰ φύλλα μάλιστ’ ἐστὶ χρήσιμα, στυπτικὴν ἔχοντα δύναμιν ἄδηκτον. ἔστι δὲ κᾀν τῷ ξηραίνειν ἐναργῶς κατὰ τὴν τρίτην που τάξιν ἀρχομένην, ὅθεν αὐτῶν τὸ ἀφέψημα καὶ δυσεντερικοῖς ἐνίεται καὶ ὠσὶ πυοροῦσι καὶ κολλητικὸς ὑπάρχει μεγάλων τραυμάτων. ἐναργέστερον δὲ ταῦτα ποιεῖ μετ’ οἴνου μέλανος αὐστηροῦ, ξηραίνει γὰρ ἰσχυρῶς ἁπάσας τὰς παρὰ φύσιν ὑγρότητας. ἔτι δὲ χλωρὰ τὰ φύλλα καταπλασσόμενα δύναμιν ἐφεκτικὴν αἱμοῤῥαγίας ἔχει καὶ τὰς ἐκ πληγῆς δὲ συγχύσεις τῶν ὀφθαλμῶν ὀνίνησι καταπλασσόμενος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συγχύσεις, καταπλασσόμενος
8.18.38 [38. On stoibe.] Of stoibe the fruit and the leaves are especially useful, having an astringent power that does not bite. It is also, in drying, evidently somewhere at the beginning of the third rank, whence their decoction is injected both for dysenterics and for ears discharging pus, and is agglutinating of large wounds. It does these things more evidently with harsh black wine, for it dries strongly all the moistures that are contrary to nature. Further, the leaves while still green, applied as a plaster, have a power of checking haemorrhage, and it also benefits the bruisings of the eyes that result from a blow when applied as a plaster.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κολλητικὸς (masculine, does not agree with the neuter ἀφέψημα; perhaps refers back to ὁ καρπός, or the text is corrupt), καταπλασσόμενος (masculine subject unclear; perhaps ὁ καρπός), συγχύσεις (rendered 'confusions'; the technical sense is an effusion or suffusion in the eye after a blow), κατὰ τὴν τρίτην που τάξιν ἀρχομένην
8.18.38 [38. On stoibē.] Of stoibē the fruit and the leaves are especially useful, having an astringent power that does not bite. It is also manifestly drying, at about the beginning of the third order. Hence their decoction is both injected for dysenterics and into suppurating ears, and it is agglutinating of large wounds. It does these things more manifestly with dark, harsh wine, for it strongly dries all moistures contrary to nature. Further, the leaves, applied green as a poultice, have a power checking haemorrhages; and applied as a poultice it also benefits the confusions of the eyes resulting from a blow.

Περὶ στοιχάδος.] Στοιχάδος ἡ μὲν πρὸς τὴν γεῦσιν ποιότης πικρά τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ ὑποστύφουσα μετρίως. ἡ δὲ κρᾶσις σύνθετος ἔκ τε ψυχρᾶς γεώδους οὐσίας ὀλίγης, ἀφ’ ἧς στύφει, καὶ λελεπτυσμένης ἑτέρας γεώδους πλείονος, ἀφ’ ἧς πικράζει. διὰ δὲ τὴν ἀμφοτέρων σύνοδον ἐκφράττειν καὶ λεπτύνειν καὶ ἀποῤῥύπτειν καὶ ῥωννύναι πέφυκε τά τε σπλάγχνα πάντα καὶ ὅλου τοῦ ζώου τὴν ἕξιν. ἐδείχθη γὰρ ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν ὡς ὅσα σύγκειται φάρμακα πρὸς τῶν τοιούτων οὐσιῶν τὰς εἰρημένας ἐνεργείας ἐνεργεῖ.

8.18.39 [39. On stoechas.] Of stoechas the quality in relation to taste is both bitter and moderately sub-astringent. Its mixture is composite, from a small amount of cold earthy substance, from which it is astringent, and from another, larger amount of thinned earthy substance, from which it is bitter. For it was shown in what precedes that all the drugs which are composed of such substances perform the said activities.

8.18.39 [39. On stoechas.] The quality of stoechas as regards taste is bitter and mildly astringent, to a moderate degree. Its mixture is composite: a small amount of cold earthy substance, from which it is astringent, and a larger amount of another earthy substance that has been thinned, from which it is bitter. Through the conjunction of both it is naturally apt to clear obstructions, to thin, to cleanse, and to strengthen all the viscera and the condition of the whole animal. For it was shown in the preceding books that all drugs composed of such substances perform the actions stated.

Περὶ σύκων.] Σῦκα τὰ μὲν ξηρὰ θερμὰ τὴν δύναμίν ἐστι, κατὰ τὴν πρώτην που τάξιν ἤδη συμπληρουμένην, ἢ τὴν δευτέραν ἀρχομένην, ἔχει δέ τι καὶ λεπτομερές. ἐξ ἀμφοῖν τούτων ἱκανὰ συμπέπτειν ἐστὶ τοὺς σκληροὺς τῶν ὄγκων, εὐθὺς δ’ αὐτοὺς διαφορεῖ. καὶ αὐτὰ μὲν οὖν καθ’ ἑαυτὰ καταπλασσόμενα τοιαύτης ἐστὶ δυνάμεως. καὶ τὸ ἀφέψημα δ’ αὐτῶν ὁμοίας ὑπάρχει φύσεως. χρὴ δὲ ὅπου καὶ συμπέψαι βούλει μᾶλλον, πύρινον ἄλευρον μιγνύειν, ὅπου δὲ διαφορῆσαι, κρίθινον. ὁ δὲ ἄρτος ἐν τῷ μέσῳ τούτων ἐστίν. ἀλλὰ μὲν τοιαῦτα τῆς περὶ φαρμάκων συνθέσεώς ἐστι καὶ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς οἰκειότερα μεθόδου. πρὸς δὲ τὸ παρὸν ἀρκεῖ καὶ ταῦτα γινώσκειν ὑπὲρ ἰσχάδων. εἰδέναι δὲ καὶ ὡς αἱ μὲν ῥυπαρώτεραι πέπτειν μᾶλλον, αἱ δριμύτεραι δ’ ἐν τῷ γεύεσθαι ῥύπτειν τε καὶ διαφορεῖν πεφύκασι, τὸ δ’ ἐπιπλεῖστον αὐτῶν ἑψηθεισῶν ἐν ὕδατι γιγνόμενον ὅμοιόν ἐστιν οὐ τὴν σύστασιν μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὴν ﻿ δύναμιν. μέλιτι. τὰ δὲ χλωρὰ σῦκα ἐσθιόμενα διὰ τὴν ἐπιμιξίαν τῆς ὑγρότητος ἀσθενεστέρας ἐστὶ δυνάμεως, ὑπάγει μέντοι κοιλίαν ἄμφω, καὶ τὰ ὑγρὰ καὶ τὰ ξηρά. τὰ δὲ τῶν ἐρινεῶν σῦκα δριμείας ἐστὶ καὶ διαφορητικῆς δυνάμεως. οὕτω δὲ καὶ τῶν ἡμέρων οἱ ὄλυνθοι, μετέχουσι γὰρ ἔτι τοῦ τῆς συκῆς ὀποῦ.

8.18.43 Figs, the dry ones, are hot in power, somewhere about the first order already being completed, or the second beginning; and they also have something fine-particled. From both of these they are capable of concocting the hard sort of swellings, and they at once disperse them. And so applied by themselves as a plaster they are of such a power; and their decoction too is of a similar nature. But where you wish rather to concoct, one should mix in wheat flour, and where to disperse, barley-meal. Bread stands midway between these. But such matters belong more properly to the composition of drugs and to the therapeutic method. For the present it suffices to know these things also about dried figs. One should know too that the dirtier ones are more apt to concoct, while those more pungent in taste are by nature cleansing and dispersing; and what results when they have been boiled in water for a very long time is similar to honey, not only in consistency but also in power. Green figs, when eaten, because of the admixture of moisture, are of weaker power; both kinds, however, the moist and the dry, move the bowel. The figs of wild fig-trees are of pungent and dispersing power. So too the unripe figs of cultivated trees, for they still partake of the juice of the fig-tree.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπιπλεῖστον, ῥυπαρώτεραι, μέλιτι (punctuation in Kühn separates it from ὅμοιόν; taken as its complement), ὄγκων, ὀποῦ
8.18.43 [43. On figs.] Of figs, the dry ones are hot in power, somewhere about the first order when it is already being completed, or the second when it is beginning; and they also have something fine-particled. From both of these [properties] they are capable of concocting hard swellings, and they also disperse them straightaway. So when applied as a poultice by themselves they are of such a power; and their decoction is also of a similar nature. Where you wish rather to concoct, you should mix in wheaten meal, and where you wish to disperse, barley [meal]. Bread is intermediate between these. But such matters belong more properly to the composition of drugs and to the therapeutic method. For the present purpose it is enough to know these things about dried figs. One should also know that the dirtier ones are more apt to concoct, while those that are more pungent to the taste are naturally disposed to be cleansing and dispersing; and what results from them when they are boiled in water for a very long time is like honey, not only in consistency but also in power. Fresh figs, when eaten, are of weaker power because of the admixture of moisture; both, however, the moist and the dry alike, move the bowel. The figs of the wild fig trees are of pungent and dispersing power. So too are the unripe figs of the cultivated trees, for they still share in the sap of the fig tree.

8.18.44 [μδ΄. Περὶ συκῆς.] Συκῆ θερμῆς καὶ λεπτομεροῦς ἐστι κράσεως, ὡς ὅ τε ὀπὸς αὐτῆς δηλοῖ καὶ τῶν φύλλων ὁ χυλός. ἰσχυρῶς γὰρ ἑκάτερον αὐτῶν ἐστι θερμὸν, ὥστε οὐδὲ δάκνει μόνον ἢ ῥύπτει σφοδρῶς, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἑλκοῖ καὶ ἀναστομοῖ καὶ μυρμηκίας ἐκβάλλει. ἔστι δὲ καὶ καθαρτικόν. ὁ δὲ τῆς ἀγρίας συκῆς, ἣν ἐρινεὸν ὀνομάζουσιν, ὀπός τε καὶ χυλὸς ἰσχυρότερος εἰς ἅπαντα τοῦ τῆς ἡμέρου. καὶ αἱ κράδαι δ’ αὐτῶν οὕτως εἰσὶ θερμαὶ καὶ λεπτομερεῖς τὴν κρᾶσιν ὥστε καὶ τοῖς βοείοις κρέασι τοῖς σκληροῖς ἑψομένοις ἐμβαλλόμεναι τακερὰ ποιοῦσιν αὐτά.

8.18.44 [44. On the fig-tree.] The fig-tree is of a hot and fine-particled mixture, as both its juice and the sap of its leaves show. For each of them is strongly hot, so that it not only bites or cleanses vigorously, but also ulcerates and opens up the orifices and casts out myrmecia. It is also purgative. The juice and the sap of the wild fig-tree, which they call erineos, are stronger for all purposes than those of the cultivated one. And their young branches (kradai) too are so hot and fine-particled in mixture that, when thrown in with tough beef as it is being boiled, they make it tender.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναστομοῖ, κράδαι, τακερὰ
8.18.44 [44. On the fig tree.] The fig tree is of a hot and fine-particled mixture, as both its sap and the juice of its leaves show. For each of them is strongly hot, so that it not only bites or cleanses vehemently, but also ulcerates, opens the mouths of vessels and removes myrmeciae. It is also purgative. The sap and juice of the wild fig tree, which they call erineos, are stronger in every respect than those of the cultivated. And their branches are so hot and fine-particled in mixture that, when thrown in with tough beef that is being boiled, they make it tender.

8.18.45 [με΄. Περὶ συμφύτου πετραίου.] Σύμφυτον πετραῖον ἐξ ἐναντίων σύγκειται δυνάμεων. ἔχει μὲν γάρ τι καὶ τμητικὸν, ᾧ καὶ τὸ περιεχόμενον ἐν θώρακι καὶ πνεύμονι πῦον ἐκκαθαίρειν πέφυκεν. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ συνακτικὸν, ᾧ καὶ πρὸς τὰς τοῦ αἵματος ἀναγωγὰς ἐπιβοηθεῖ, καὶ τρίτην ἐπ’ αὐτοῖς ὑγρότητά τινα θερμὴν οὐκ ἀμέτρως, δι’ ἣν καὶ γλυκὺ φαίνεται γευομένοις καὶ ἡδὺ πρὸς τὴν ὄσφρησιν. ἄδιψον δ’ ἐστὶ διαμασώμενον καὶ τὰς ἐν ἀρτηρίᾳ τραχύτητας ἰᾶται. κατὰ δὲ τὴν ἐξ ἁπασῶν τῶν εἰρημένων δυνάμεων μίξιν ἅμα τε καὶ διαφορεῖν ἱκανόν ἐστιν καὶ συνάγειν καὶ σφίγγειν τὰ σώματα. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ταῖς ἐντεροκήλαις ἐπιτίθεται καὶ πρὸς σπάσματα καὶ ῥήγματα σὺν ὀξυμέλιτι πίνεται. ὅσοι δ’ αὐτὸ καὶ πρὸς δυσεντερίαν καὶ ῥοῦν ἐρυθρὸν, ἕψοντες ἐν οἴνῳ διδόασιν, ὡς ξηραίνοντί τε καὶ συνάγοντι χρῶνται, καὶ ὅσοι πρὸς νεφρῖτιν, ὡς ἐκκαθαίροντι καὶ τέμνοντι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥοῦν ἐρυθρὸν
8.18.45 [45. On rock comfrey.] Rock comfrey is composed of opposite powers. For it has something cutting, by which it is naturally fitted to clear out the pus contained in the thorax and lung; and it has something contracting, by which it also helps against bringings-up of blood; and, third in addition to these, a certain moisture that is warm, not immoderately so, on account of which it also appears sweet to those who taste it and pleasant to the sense of smell. Chewed, it allays thirst, and it cures the roughnesses in the windpipe. In accordance with the mixture of all the powers mentioned, it is capable at once both of dispersing and of contracting and binding bodies. And for this reason it is both applied to intestinal hernias and drunk with oxymel for sprains and ruptures. Those who give it also for dysentery and red flux, boiling it in wine, use it as a drying and contracting agent, and those who give it for kidney disease, as a cleansing and cutting one.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀναγωγὰς, σπάσματα, ἀρτηρίᾳ, τρίτην
8.18.45 [45. On rock symphyton.] Rock symphyton is composed of opposite powers. For it has something cutting, by which it is also naturally able to clear out the pus contained in the thorax and lung. It also has something that draws together, by which it also gives help against the bringing-up of blood. And, third, besides these, it has a certain moisture that is warm in no immoderate degree, on account of which it also appears sweet to those who taste it and pleasant to the sense of smell. When chewed it allays thirst, and it heals roughnesses in the windpipe (arteria). In accordance with the mixture of all the powers mentioned, it is able at the same time both to disperse and to draw together and to constrict bodies. For this reason it is both applied to enteroceles and drunk with oxymel for strains and ruptures. Those who give it, boiled in wine, for dysentery and red flux use it as something that dries and draws together; and those who give it for nephritis use it as something that clears out and cuts.

8.18.48 [48. On schinos (mastic tree).] Schinos (mastic tree): this shrub is composed of a watery substance gently warm in its power and of an earthy cold one, not in large quantity, on account of which it is also moderately astringent. It dries, then, in the second order when already being completed, or in the third when beginning. In the difference according to heat and cold, it is somewhat intermediate and well-balanced. It has a similar astringency in all its own parts: in roots, in branches, in young shoots, in sprouts, in leaves, and further in fruit and bark. And if you should squeeze out juice from its green leaves, this too is of a similar order, moderately astringent, whence it is also drunk both by itself and together with the other drugs, as many as heal dysentery and coeliac conditions. And indeed it is also suitable for spittings of blood and haemorrhages from the womb and prolapses of the seat and of the uterus, being something close to hypocistis.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καθ’ ἑαυτήν (feminine, although χυλός is masculine; possibly corrupt for καθ’ ἑαυτόν), ὁμοίαν δὲ ἐν ἅπασιν ἔχει τοῖς ἑαυτοῦ μέρεσι· τὴν στύψιν (the punctuation before τὴν στύψιν is odd; taken as one clause), ἐπιτήδειός ἐστιν (subject unclear: the juice or the shrub)
8.18.48 [48. On mastic.] Mastic: this shrub is composed of a watery substance gently hot in power and of an earthy, cold substance, not in great quantity, on account of which it is also moderately astringent. It dries, then, in the second order when this is already being completed, or at the beginning of the third. In the distinction according to heat and cold it is in some way intermediate and well-balanced. It has a similar astringency in all its parts: in the roots, in the branches, in the shoots, in the sprouts, in the leaves, and further in the fruit and the bark. And if you squeeze out juice from its leaves while green, this too is of a similar order, being moderately astringent; hence it is drunk both on its own and together with the other drugs that cure dysentery and coeliac affections. And indeed it is also suitable for spittings of blood, haemorrhages from the uterus, and prolapses of the seat and of the womb, being somewhat close to hypocistis.

8.18.49 [μθ΄. Περὶ σχίνου ἄνθους.] Σχίνου ἄνθος θερμαίνει μετρίως καὶ στύφει μετρίως ἔτι καὶ τῆς λεπτομεροῦς φύσεως οὐκ ἀπήλλακται. τοιγάρτοι διὰ ταῦτα καὶ οὐρητικόν ἐστι τὸ φάρμακον καὶ καταμηνίων ἀγωγὸν, ἐν πυρίαις τε καὶ πόμασι παραλαμβανόμενον. ὠφελεῖ δὲ καὶ τὰς καθ’ ἧπαρ καὶ στόμαχον καὶ κοιλίαν φλεγμονάς. ἔστι δ’ αὐτοῦ στυπτικωτέρα μὲν ἡ ῥίζα, τὸ δὲ καλούμενον ἄνθος θερμότερον. ἐν ἅπασι δὲ τοῖς μορίοις αὐτοῦ, τοῖς μὲν μᾶλλον, τοῖς δ’ ἧττον, ἐμφαίνεταί τι γευόμενον στύψεως, δι’ ἣν καὶ πρὸς τοῖς αἱμοπτυϊκοῖς διδομένοις μίγνυται φαρμάκοις.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.18.49 [49. On the flower of schinos (mastic tree).] The flower of schinos heats moderately and is moderately astringent, and furthermore it is not devoid of the fine-particled nature. Accordingly, for these reasons the drug is also diuretic and promotive of the menses, being employed both in fomentations and in draughts. It also benefits the inflammations in the liver, the stomach and the belly. Of it the root is more astringent, while what is called the flower is hotter. In all its parts, in some more, in others less, something of astringency shows itself on tasting, on account of which it is also mixed with the drugs given to those who spit blood.

8.18.49 [49. On the flower of mastic.] The flower of mastic heats moderately and is moderately astringent; moreover, it has not been deprived of its fine-particled nature. Accordingly, for these reasons the drug is both diuretic and a promoter of the menses, when employed in fomentations and in potions. It also benefits inflammations in the liver, the stomach and the belly. Its root is more astringent, while the so-called flower is hotter. In all its parts, more in some and less in others, something of astringency shows itself on tasting, and on account of this it is also mixed into the drugs given to those who spit blood.

Περὶ σχοίνου λείας, ὀξυσχοίνου καὶ ὁλοσχοίνου.] Σχοίνου ἡ μὲν λεία, ἡ δ’ ὀξύσχοινος, ἡ δ’ ὁλόσχοινος ὀνομάζεται. ἰσχνοτέρα μὲν καὶ σκληροτέρα ἡ ὀξύσχοινος, παχυτέρα δὲ καὶ χαυνοτέρα ἡ ὁλόσχοινος. ὁ καρπὸς δὲ τῆς ὁλοσχοίνου μὲν ὑπνωτικὸς, τῆς δ’ ὀξυσχοίνου δύο εἰσὶν εἴδη· τὸ μὲν ἄκαρπον, οὗ χρῆσις οὐδεμία πρὸς τὰς ἰάσεις, τὸ δ’ ἕτερον καρποφόρον· ὑπνώδης δὲ καὶ ταύτης ὁ καρπὸς, ἀλλ’ ἧττον ἢ τῆς ὁλοσχοίνου. ἔστι μὴν οὗτος καὶ κεφαλαλγής. ἀμφότεροι δὲ εἰ φρυγέντες μετ’ οἴνου πίνοιντο, τὰ κατὰ τὴν γαστέρα ξηραίνουσι ῥεύματα καὶ ῥοῦν τὸν ἐρυθρὸν ἐπέχουσιν. ἐξ ὧν δῆλον ὡς ἡ κρᾶσις αὐτῶν ἐστι σύνθετος ἐκ γεώδους οὐσίας ἀτρέμα ψυχρᾶς καὶ ὑδατώδους ἀτρέμα θερμῆς, ἵνα ξηραίνειν τε τὰ κάτω δύνωνται καὶ πρὸς τὴν κεφαλὴν ἀτμοὺς ἀναπέμπειν ἠρέμα ψυχροὺς, οἷς καὶ ὑπνώδεις ἐργάζονται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.18.50 [50. On smooth rush, sharp rush and whole rush.] Of rush, one kind is called smooth, another sharp rush, another whole rush. The sharp rush is thinner and harder, the whole rush thicker and more spongy. The fruit of the whole rush is sleep-inducing; of the sharp rush there are two kinds: the one fruitless, of which there is no use for cures, the other fruit-bearing; and the fruit of this too is soporific, but less so than that of the whole rush. This one, indeed, is also productive of headache. Both, if roasted and drunk with wine, dry up the fluxes in the belly and check the red flux. From which it is clear that their mixture is composed of an earthy substance that is gently cold and a watery one that is gently hot, so that they are able both to dry the lower parts and to send up to the head vapours that are mildly cold, by which they also render people soporific.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χαυνοτέρα, ἔστι μὴν οὗτος καὶ κεφαλαλγής (referent of οὗτος: the fruit of the oxyschoinos, or the fruit-bearing kind), ὑπνώδεις ἐργάζονται (object unexpressed: 'make [people] drowsy' or 'prove soporific'), ῥεύματα
8.18.50 [50. On smooth rush, oxyschoenus and holoschoenus.] Of rush (schoinos) one kind is called smooth, another oxyschoinos, and another holoschoinos. The oxyschoinos is slenderer and harder, while the holoschoinos is thicker and more porous. The fruit of the holoschoinos is sleep-inducing. Of the oxyschoinos there are two kinds: the one fruitless, which has no use for cures, the other fruit-bearing. The fruit of this one too is soporific, but less so than that of the holoschoinos. This fruit is, however, also productive of headache. Both, if they are roasted and drunk with wine, dry the fluxions in the belly and check the red flux. From this it is clear that their mixture is compounded of an earthy substance that is mildly cold and a watery one that is mildly hot, so that they are able both to dry the parts below and to send up to the head mildly cold vapours, by which they also prove soporific.

8.19.1 [1. On the terebinth.] Of the terebinth both the bark and the leaves and the fruit have something astringent. But they also heat in the second order, so that it is already clear that they also dry: while they are still fresh and moist, moderately, but once they have been dried, in the second order. 8.19.2 [2. On beet.] Beet partakes of a certain nitrous power, by which it both cleanses and disperses and purges through the nostrils. 8.19.4 [δ΄. Περὶ τέφρας.] Τέφρα. τῶν κεκαυμένων ξύλων τὸ λείψανον οὕτω προσαγορεύεται, σύνθετον ὑπάρχον ἐξ ἐναντίων οὐσιῶν τε καὶ ποιοτήτων. ἔχει γὰρ ἐν αὑτῷ τὸ μέν τι γεῶδες, τὸ δ’ οἷον αἰθαλῶδες ἢ λιγνυῶδες ἢ ὅπως ἂν ἐθέλῃ τις καλεῖν. ταυτὶ μὲν οὖν τὰ μόρια λεπτομερῆ τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ βρεχομένης ὕδατι τῆς τέφρας καὶ διηθουμένης συναποφέρεται. ὅσον δ’ ὑπολείπεται γεῶδες, ἀσθενὲς καὶ ἄδηκτον γίγνεται ἐν τῇ κονίᾳ τὴν θερμὴν δύναμιν ἀποτιθέμενον. οὐχ ἅπασα δὲ τέφρα τὴν αὐτὴν ἀκριβῶς ἔχει κρᾶσιν, ἀλλὰ κατὰ τὴν τῆς καυθείσης ὕλης διαφορὰν ὑπαλλάττεται. Διοσκορίδης δὲ οὐκ οἶδ’ ὅπως στυπτικὴν αὐτὴν ἔχειν φησὶ τὴν δύναμιν. καίτοι γε ἡ συκίνη πάσης ἀπήλλακται τοιαύτης ποιότητος, ὅτι καὶ αὐτὸ τὸ δένδρον οὐχ ὥσπερ δρῦς καὶ πρῖνος καὶ κόμαρος καὶ φηγὸς καὶ σχῖνος καὶ κισσὸς, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα, τὴν στρυφνὴν ἐπιφαίνει ποιότητα κατ’ οὐδὲν ἑαυτοῦ μέρος, ἀλλ’ ἔστιν ὀποῦ πλῆρες ὅλον ἰσχυροῦ καὶ θερμοῦ καὶ δριμέος. ἐκ μὲν δὴ τῶν στρυφνῶν ξύλων ἡ τέφρα στυπτικὸν οὐκ ὀλίγον ἔχει, καὶ ἔγωγέ ποτε δι’ αὐτῆς ἐπισχὼν αἱμοῤῥαγίας οἶδα, μηδενὸς ἑτέρου παρόντος φαρμάκου. τῇ συκίνῃ δ’ οὐκ ἄν τις εἰς τοῦτο χρήσαιτό ποτε, πολὺ γὰρ αὕτη γε τὸ δριμὺ καὶ τὸ καυστικὸν ἔχει τῷ ῥυπτικῷ μεμιγμένον καὶ κατ’ ἄμφω διενήνοχε τῆς ἐκ τῶν δρυΐνων ξύλων, ὅτι τε τὸ αἰθαλῶδες ἐν αὐτῇ πολλῷ δριμύτερόν ἐστι καὶ ὅτι τὸ οἷον γεῶδες, ἐν ἐκείναις μὲν ὑποστῦφόν πώς ἐστιν, ἐν ταύτῃ δὲ ῥυπτικὸν, ὥσπερ ἐν τῇ τῶν τιθυμάλλων. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἡ τίτανος εἶδός τι τέφρας, λεπτομερεστέρα μὲν οὖσα τῆς ἐκ τῶν ξύλων, παρ’ ὅσον ἀκριβέστερον οἱ λίθοι κατοπτᾶσθαι δέονται πρὸς τὸ γενέσθαι τέφραν, ὅμως μὴν ἔχουσα καὶ αὐτὴ τὸ οἷον ἐμπύρευμα πολύ. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πλυθεῖσα ξηραντικὸν ἀδήκτως γίνεται φάρμακον, καὶ μᾶλλον, εἰ δὶς ἢ καὶ τρὶς πλυθείη. διαφορητικὴ δ’ ἱκανῶς γίνεται θαλάσσῃ πλυθεῖσα. λεχθήσεται δὲ ὑπὲρ αὐτῆς κᾀπειδὰν περὶ τῶν μεταλλευομένων ὁ λόγος ἡμῖν γίγνηται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λιγνυῶδες, ἐμπύρευμα
8.19.4 [4. On ash.] Ash. The residue of burnt wood is so called, being compounded out of opposite substances and qualities. For it has in itself on the one hand something earthy, on the other something as it were sooty or smoke-like, or whatever one wishes to call it. Now these parts are fine-particled, and when the ash is soaked in water and filtered through they are carried off along with it. But what earthy part is left behind becomes weak and non-biting, having deposited its hot power in the lye. Not all ash, however, has exactly the same mixture, but it varies according to the difference of the material that has been burnt. Dioscorides, though, says—I do not know how—that it has an astringent power. And yet fig-wood ash is free of every such quality, because the tree itself too, unlike oak and holm-oak and strawberry-tree and valonia oak and mastic-tree and ivy and all other such trees, displays the acerbic quality in no part of itself, but is wholly full of a strong, hot and pungent juice. From acerbic woods, then, the ash has no small astringent element, and I myself know that I once checked haemorrhages by means of it, when no other drug was at hand. But fig-wood ash no one would ever use for this purpose, for this one has much of the pungent and the caustic mixed with the cleansing, and in both respects it differs from the ash from oak woods, both because the sooty element in it is far more pungent and because the earthy element, as it were, is in those somewhat sub-astringent, whereas in this it is cleansing, as it is in the ash of the spurges (tithymalloi). Lime too is a kind of ash, being more fine-particled than that from woods, inasmuch as stones need to be roasted more thoroughly in order to become ash, yet nevertheless it too has much of the fiery residue (empyreuma), as it were. And for this reason, when washed, it becomes a drying drug without biting, and the more so if it is washed twice or even three times. It becomes sufficiently dispersing when washed with sea-water. It will be spoken of also when our discussion comes to deal with the minerals.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: λιγνυῶδες, φηγὸς, ἐμπύρευμα, ὑποστῦφόν
8.19.4 [4. On ash.] Ash. The residue of burnt wood is called by this name; it is compounded of contrary substances and qualities. For it has in itself on the one hand something earthy, and on the other something as it were sooty or fuliginous, or however one may wish to call it. These parts, then, are fine-particled, and when the ash is wetted with water and strained they are carried off along with it. But whatever earthy part is left behind becomes weak and non-biting, having deposited its hot power in the lye. Not all ash has exactly the same mixture; rather, it varies according to the difference of the material burnt. Dioscorides, I do not know how, says that it has an astringent power. Yet the ash of fig-wood is free of every such quality, because the tree itself, unlike oak, holm-oak, arbutus, phēgos-oak, mastic and ivy, and all the others of this kind, does not display the acerbic quality in any part of itself, but is wholly full of a strong, hot and pungent juice. From acerbic woods, then, the ash has no little astringency, and I myself know that I once stopped haemorrhages by means of it, when no other drug was at hand. But no one would ever use fig-wood ash for this purpose, for it has much of the pungent and the caustic mixed with the cleansing, and in both respects it differs from the ash of oak woods: both because the sooty part in it is much more pungent, and because the as it were earthy part, which in those is in some way mildly astringent, is in this one cleansing, as in the ash of spurges (tithymalloi). Quicklime (titanos) too is a kind of ash, being more fine-particled than the ash from woods, inasmuch as the stones need to be more thoroughly roasted in order to become ash; nevertheless it too has much of what one might call fiery residue. And for this reason, when washed, it becomes a drug that is drying without biting, and the more so if it is washed twice or even three times. It becomes sufficiently dispersing when washed with sea water. It will be spoken of also when our account comes to the mined substances.

Περὶ τηλεφίου.] Τηλέφιον ξηραντικῆς ἐστι καὶ ῥυπτικῆς δυνάμεως, οὐ μὴν ἐπιφανῶς γε θερμῆς, ἀλλὰ κατὰ τοῦτ’ ἄν τις ἴσως αὐτὸ θείη τῆς πρώτης τάξεως. ξηραίνει μέντοι κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν ἐπιτεταμένην, ἢ τὰ πρῶτα τῆς τρίτης. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πρός τε τὰ σηπεδονώδη τῶν ἑλκῶν ἁρμόττει καὶ λεύκας καὶ ἀλφοὺς ἰᾶται σὺν ὄξει.

8.19.5 [5. On telephion.] Telephion is of a drying and cleansing power, not however of a manifestly hot one; rather, in this respect one might perhaps place it in the first order. 8.19.7 [ζ΄. Περὶ τιθυμάλλων.] Τιθύμαλλοι πάντες ἐπικρατοῦσαν μὲν ἔχουσι τὴν δριμεῖαν καὶ θερμὴν δύναμιν. ὑπάρχει δ’ αὐτοῖς καὶ πικρότης. ἰσχυρότατος μὲν οὖν αὐτῆς ὁ ὀπὸς, ἐφεξῆς δὲ ὅ τε καρπὸς καὶ τὰ φύλλα. μετέχει δὲ καὶ ἡ ῥίζα τῶν εἰρημένων δυνάμεων, ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἐπίσης. αὐτὴ μὲν οὖν ἑψομένη σὺν ὄξει τὰ τῶν ὀδόντων ἀλγήματα καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅσα βεβρωμένοις αὐτοῖς γίνεται θεραπεύει. οἱ δὲ ὀποὶ σφοδροτέραν ἔχοντες δύναμιν εἰς μὲν τὸ τρῆμα τῶν ὀδόντων ἐντίθενται· τῶν δ’ ἄλλων τοῦ σώματος εἰ ἅψαιντο μορίων εὐθὺς ἐπικαίουσί τε καὶ ἕλκουσι, διόπερ ἔξωθεν αὐτοῖς περιπλάσσεται κηρός. εἰσὶ γὰρ ἐκ τῆς τετάρτης τάξεως τῶν θερμαινόντων, ἣν δὴ τῶν καυστικῶν ἔφαμεν ὑπάρχειν. οὕτως δὲ καὶ τὰς τρίχας ἀφαιρεῖ περιχριόμενος ὁ ὀπός. ἐπεὶ δὲ σφοδρότερός ἐστιν, ἐλαίῳ μίγνυται· καὶ εἰ πολλάκις τοῦτο γένοιτο, τελέως αἱ ῥίζαι τῶν τριχῶν ἀπόλλυνται καυθεῖσαι καὶ ψιλὸν αὐτῶν γίγνεται τὸ σῶμα. κατὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν δύναμιν ἀκροχορδόνας καὶ μυρμηκίας καὶ πτερύγια καὶ θύμους ἀφαιροῦσιν. ἀποῤῥύπτουσι δὲ καὶ λειχῆνας καὶ ψώρας, ὅτι καὶ τῆς ῥυπτικῆς αὐτοῖς μέτεστι δυνάμεως διὰ τὴν πικρότητα καὶ τὰ φαγεδαινικά τε τῶν ἑλκῶν καὶ τὰ ἀνθρακώδη καὶ τὰ γαγγραινώδη, διότι θερμαίνουσί τε σφοδρῶς καὶ ῥύπτουσιν, ὠφελεῖν πεφύκασιν, ἄν τις ἐν καιρῷ καὶ μέτρῳ χρώμενος αὐτοῖς τύχῃ. καὶ γὰρ οὖν καὶ συρίγγων τοὺς τύλους κατὰ τὴν αὐτὴν δύναμιν ἐξαίρουσιν. ἅπαντα δὲ ταῦτα κατὰ γένος μὲν ὁμοίως, ἀσθενέστερον δὲ καὶ τὰ φύλλα καὶ ὁ καρπὸς ἐργάζεσθαι πεφύκασιν. οἷς δὴ καὶ πρὸς τοὺς ἐν τοῖς στασίμοις ὕδασιν ἰχθύας εἰώθασι χρῆσθαι. τάχιστα γὰρ ὑπ’ αὐτῶν σκοτωθέντες τε καὶ ἡμίθνητοι γενόμενοι, πρὸς τὴν ἐπιφάνειαν ἀναφέρονται τοῦ ὕδατος. ἑπτὰ δὲ ὄντων αὐτῶν ἰσχυρότατος μὲν ὅ τε χαρακίας ὀνομαζόμενος, ὃν δὴ καὶ ﻿ ἄῤῥενα προσαγορεύουσιν ἔνιοι καὶ ὁ θῆλυς ὁ μυρσινίτης καλούμενος καὶ ὁ ἐν ταῖς πέτραις ὁ δενδροειδής. ἐφεξῆς δὲ ὅ τε τῇ φλόμῳ προσεοικὼς καὶ ὁ κυπαρισσίας, εἶθ’ οὕτως ὁ παράλιος, εἶτα ὁ ἡλιοσκόπος. κατὰ ἀναλογίαν δὲ τῆς εἰρημένης αὐτῶν δυνάμεως ἡ τέφρα καὶ ἡ ἀπ’ αὐτῆς κονία.

8.19.7 [7. On spurges (tithymalloi).] All spurges (tithymalloi) have as their predominant power the pungent and hot one; but there belongs to them also a bitterness. The strongest in this is the juice, and next come the fruit and the leaves. The root too shares in the powers mentioned, but not equally. Now the root itself, boiled with vinegar, cures pains of the teeth, and especially those that arise when the teeth are eaten away. The juices, which have a more vigorous power, are inserted into the hole of the teeth; but if they touch the other parts of the body they at once burn and ulcerate them, which is why wax is plastered around them on the outside. For they belong to the fourth order of heating things, which indeed we said is that of the caustics. In this way too the juice, smeared around, removes the hairs; but since it is more vigorous, it is mixed with oil, and if this is done many times, the roots of the hairs are completely destroyed by being burnt and the body becomes bare of them. By the same power they remove acrochordons, myrmecia, pterygia and thymoi. They also cleanse away lichens and psorai, because they share in the cleansing power too on account of their bitterness; and phagedaenic ulcers, and those of the anthrax kind and the gangrenous, because they heat vigorously and cleanse, they are naturally fitted to benefit, if one happens to use them at the right time and in due measure. For indeed they also remove the calluses of fistulas by the same power. All these things both the leaves and the fruit are naturally fitted to do, similarly in kind but more weakly; and these people are accustomed to use also against the fish in standing waters, for very quickly, stupefied by them and rendered half-dead, they are brought up to the surface of the water. There being seven of them, the strongest are the one named charakias, which indeed some people also call the male, and the female, called myrsinites, and the tree-like one that grows among the rocks; next the one resembling phlomos (mullein) and the kyparissias, then in this order the paralios, then the helioskopos. In proportion to the power of theirs that has been described are the ash and the lye made from it.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αὐτῆς (in ἰσχυρότατος μὲν οὖν αὐτῆς ὁ ὀπός: feminine singular, perhaps referring to δύναμις or the plant; αὐτῶν would be expected), ἕλκουσι (accented as from ἕλκω 'draw'; rendered 'ulcerate' as if ἑλκοῦσι from ἑλκόω), αὐτοῖς περιπλάσσεται κηρός (whether the wax is moulded around the juices or around the teeth), σκοτωθέντες (rendered 'stunned'; literally 'darkened, made dizzy'), θύμους
8.19.7 [7. On spurges.] All spurges (tithymalloi) have the pungent and hot power predominant, and there is also bitterness in them. The strongest of it is the juice (opos); next come both the fruit and the leaves. The root too shares in the powers mentioned, but not equally. The root, then, boiled with vinegar, cures pains of the teeth, and especially those that arise when the teeth are eaten away. The juices, having a more vehement power, are put into the hole of the teeth; but if they touch the other parts of the body they at once burn them and ulcerate them, which is why wax is moulded around them on the outside. For they belong to the fourth order of heating things, which, as we said, is that of the caustic things. In this way the juice also removes the hairs when smeared around. But since it is more vehement, it is mixed with oil; and if this is done often, the roots of the hairs are completely destroyed by being burnt, and the body becomes bare of them. By the same power they remove acrochordones, myrmeciae, pterygia and thymi. They also cleanse away leichenes and psorai, because they also share in the cleansing power on account of their bitterness. And they are naturally apt to benefit phagedaenic ulcers, carbuncular ones and gangrenous ones, because they both heat vehemently and cleanse, provided one uses them at the right time and in the right measure. And indeed by the same power they also remove the callosities of fistulae (syringes). All these effects the leaves and the fruit are naturally apt to produce in the same way as to kind, but more weakly. People are also accustomed to use these against the fish in standing waters; for the fish, very quickly stunned by them and made half-dead, are carried up to the surface of the water. There being seven of them, the strongest are the one named characias, which some people also call the male, the female one called myrsinites, and the tree-like one that grows on rocks; next the one resembling mullein (phlomos), and the cyparissias; after these the coastal one, then the helioscopos. In proportion to their stated power are the ash and the lye made from it.

8.19.8 [η΄. Περὶ τραγακάνθης.] Τραγάκανθα παραπλησίαν ἔχει τῷ κόμμι δύναμιν, ἐμπλαστικήν τέ τινα καὶ δριμύτητα ἀμβλυωτέραν. καὶ δὴ καὶ ξηραίνει παραπλησίως ἐκείνῳ. 8.19.9 [θ΄. Περὶ τραγίου.] Τραγίου καὶ τὰ φύλλα καὶ ὁ καρπὸς καὶ τὸ δάκρυον ἑλκτικῆς τε καὶ διαφορητικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως. ἔστι δὲ καὶ λεπτομερὲς καὶ θερμὸν τὴν δύναμιν ἐκ τῆς τρίτης ἤδη που τάξεως κατ’ ἀρχάς. σκόλοπάς τε συνάγει καὶ λίθους θρύπτει καὶ καταμήνια κινεῖ δραχμῆς ὁλκῇ πινόμενον. γεννᾶται δ’ ἐν Κρήτῃ μόνῃ τοῦτο τὸ τράγιον ἐοικὸς σχίνῳ. τὸ δ’ ἕτερον τράγιον τὸ τούτου μικρότερον, οὗ τὰ φύλλα τῷ σκολοπενδρίῳ προσέοικε, φαίνεται μὲν πολλαχόθι, στυπτικῆς δ’ οὐκ ὀλίγης μετέχον δυνάμεως, ὡς καὶ πρὸς τὰ ῥοώδη τῶν παθημάτων ἁρμόττειν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σκόλοπάς τε συνάγει
8.19.8 [8. On tragacanth.] Tragacanth has a power similar to that of gum, a certain clogging quality and a rather blunted pungency. And indeed it also dries in much the same way as that does. 8.19.9 [9. On tragion.] Of tragion, the leaves, the fruit and the tear (gum) are of a drawing and dispersing power. It is also fine-particled and hot in power, already somewhere at the beginning of the third rank. It draws out splinters, breaks up stones, and moves the menses when drunk in the weight of one drachm. This tragion, which resembles the schinos (mastic tree), grows only in Crete. The other tragion, the one smaller than this, whose leaves resemble those of scolopendrion, appears in many places, and it partakes of no small astringent power, so that it is suitable also for the fluxional affections.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀμβλυωτέραν, ἤδη που, σκολοπενδρίῳ, τράγιον

It draws out splinters, breaks up stones, and moves the menses when drunk in the weight of a drachm. This tragion grows in Crete alone and resembles mastic. The other tragion, which is smaller than this one and whose leaves resemble skolopendrion, appears in many places; it shares in no small astringent power, so that it is also suited to fluxional affections.

8.19.10 [10. On tragoriganon.] Tragoriganon: it has been discussed earlier, in the account of Origanum. 8.19.11 [11. On tribolos.] Tribolos is composed of a moist substance that is moderately cold and of a dry one that is, for its part, not moderately cold. In the land kind the earthy cold element prevails, which we have also shown to be astringent, while in the water kind the watery element prevails; and for this reason both are suitable both against the beginnings of inflammations and, in general, against all fluxes. The fruit of the land kind, being fine-particled, breaks up stones in the kidneys when drunk.

On caltrop.] Caltrop is composed of a moist substance that is moderately cold and of a dry substance, this too cold, and not moderately so. Περὶ τριφύλλου.] Τρίφυλλον, οἱ δὲ ἀσφάλτιον, οἱ δ’ ὀξύφυλλον, ἔνιοι δὲ μινυανθὲς, εἰσὶ δ’ οἳ κνίκιον ὀνομάζουσιν· ἀπὸ μὲν τῶν συμβεβηκότων τῷ θάμνῳ τὰ πρῶτα τρία, τὰ δὲ ὑπόλοιπα δύο οὐκ οἶδ’ ὁπόθεν. ἔστι δὲ ἡ δύναμις τοῦ φυτοῦ θερμὴ καὶ ξηρὰ κατὰ τὴν ἄσφαλτον, ᾗ παραπλήσιόν ἐστι κατὰ τὴν ὀσμὴν, τῆς τρίτης τάξεως κατ’ ἄμφω. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πινόμενον καὶ πλευρῶν ἀλγήματα ἐπ’ ἐμφράξεσιν ὀνίνησι καὶ οὖρα κινεῖ καὶ καταμήνια.

8.19.12 [12. On tripolion.] Tripolion. The root is pungent to the taste and hot in its power to the third degree of remove. 8.19.13 [13. On trefoil (triphyllon).] Trefoil (triphyllon); some call it asphaltion, others oxyphyllon, some people minyanthes, and there are those who name it knikion. The first three names derive from attributes of the shrub; as for the remaining two, I do not know from where they come. The power of the plant is hot and dry, after the manner of asphalt, to which it is similar in smell, being of the third order in both respects. And for this reason, when drunk, it also relieves pains of the sides arising from obstructions, and it moves the urine and the menses.

8.19.14 [ιδ΄. Περὶ τριχομανοῦς.] Τριχομανὲς ὅσα περ ἀδίαντον δύναται. 8.19.15 [ιε΄. Περὶ τρύχνου ἢ στρύχνου.] Τρύχνον, ἔνιοι δὲ μετὰ τοῦ σίγμα στρύχνον ὀνομάζουσι. τὸ μὲν ἐδώδιμον ἐν τοῖς κήποις φυόμενον ἅπαντες γινώσκουσι καὶ χρῶνται πρὸς ὅσα ψῦξαί τε καὶ στῦψαι δέονται. δύναται γὰρ ἄμφω ταῦτα κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν τάξιν. τῶν δ’ ἄλλων τῶν οὐκ ἐδωδίμων τὸ μὲν ἁλικάκαβον ὀνομάζεται, πυῤῥὸν ἔχον τὸν καρπὸν ἐοικότα ῥαγὶ σταφυλῆς κατὰ τὸ σχῆμα καὶ τὸ μέγεθος, ᾧ καὶ πρὸς τοὺς στεφάνους χρῶνται. τὸ δ’ ἕτερον τὸ θαμνῶδες τὸ ὑπνωτικὸν καὶ τρίτον ἐστὶν ἐπ’ αὐτοῖς τὸ μανικόν. τὸ μὲν οὖν ἁλικάκαβον ἐν τῇ τῶν φύλλων δυνάμει παραπλήσιον ὑπάρχει τῷ κηπευομένῳ τρύχνῳ, τὸν καρπὸν οὐρητικὸν ἔχον. διὸ καὶ πολλαῖς τῶν συνθέτων δυνάμεων, ὅσαι πρὸς ἧπαρ ἢ κύστιν ἢ νεφροὺς ἁρμόττουσι, μίγνυται. τοῦ δ’ ὑπνωτικοῦ στρύχνου τῆς ῥίζης ὁ φλοιὸς μετ’ οἴνου πινόμενος ὑπνωτικὸς ὑπάρχει, δραχμῆς σταθμῷ λαμβανόμενος. ἔστι δὲ καὶ εἰς τὰ ἄλλα τῷ τοῦ μήκωνος ὀπῷ παραπλήσιος, ἀσθενέστερος δ’ εἰς τοσοῦτον ὡς τῆς τρίτης εἶναι τάξεως τῶν ψυχόντων, ἐκείνου κατὰ τὴν τετάρτην τεταγμένου. τὸ σπέρμα τούτου τοῦ τρύχνου δύναμιν οὐρητικὴν ἔχει. μανιῶδες δ’ ἐστὶ, πλέον τῶν δώδεκα κορύμβων πινόμενον. τὸ δ’ ὑπόλοιπον τῶν εἰρημένων τρύχνων ἄχρηστόν ἐστιν εἰς τὰς τῶν ἔνδον ἰάσεις. εἰ μὲν γὰρ τέτταρες δραχμαὶ ληφθεῖεν αὐτῶν, θάνατον ἐπιφέρουσιν, εἰ δ’ ἐλάττους, μανίαν. ἄλυπος δ’ ἡ μία, πλὴν οὐδ’ αὐτή τι χρηστὸν ἔχουσα. ἔξωθεν δὲ τοῦ σώματος ἐπιπλασσομένου ἕλκη κακοήθη καὶ νομώδη θεραπεύει. ἄριστος δ’ εἰς τοσοῦτον τῆς ῥίζης ὁ φλοιὸς, ξηραίνων μὲν κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν που τάξιν ἤδη συμπληρουμένην καὶ τρίτην ἀρχομένην, ψύχων δὲ κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν ἀρχομένην.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔξωθεν δὲ τοῦ σώματος ἐπιπλασσομένου, ἄριστος δ’ εἰς τοσοῦτον, κορύμβων
8.19.14 [14. On trichomanes.] Trichomanes has the same powers as adianton. 8.19.15 [15. On trychnon or strychnon.] Trychnon: some people name it with the sigma, strychnon. The edible kind, which grows in gardens, everyone knows and uses for all conditions that need to be cooled and made astringent; for it has both these powers in the second order. Of the others, which are not edible, one is called halikakabon, having a red fruit resembling a grape-berry in shape and size, which they also use for garlands; the other is the shrub-like, sleep-inducing kind; and third after these is the madness-inducing kind. Now the halikakabon is similar in the power of its leaves to the garden-grown trychnon, and has a diuretic fruit; and for this reason it is also mixed into many of the compound remedies that are suited to the liver or bladder or kidneys. Of the sleep-inducing strychnon, the bark of the root, drunk with wine, is sleep-inducing, taken in the weight of one drachm. In other respects too it is similar to the juice of the poppy, but weaker to such a degree that it belongs to the third order of cooling things, whereas that one is placed in the fourth. The seed of this trychnon has a diuretic power; but it is madness-producing when more than twelve clusters are drunk. The remaining one of the trychna mentioned is useless for the cures of internal parts. For if four drachms of them are taken, they bring on death; if fewer, madness. A single drachm is harmless, except that even this has nothing useful in it. But when it is plastered on the body externally, it cures malignant and spreading ulcers. Best for this purpose is the bark of the root, drying at about the second order already being completed and the third beginning, and cooling at the second order beginning.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπιπλασσομένου, κορύμβων, εἰς τοσοῦτον, αὐτῶν
8.19.14 [14. On trichomanes.] Trichomanes has all the same powers as adiantum. 8.19.15 [15. On trychnon or strychnon.] Trychnon, which some people call strychnon, with the sigma. Everyone knows the edible kind that grows in gardens, and people use it for whatever needs cooling and astringent action, for it has both these powers in the second order. Of the other kinds, which are not edible, one is called halicacabum. It has a reddish fruit resembling a grape berry in shape and size, and people also use it for garlands. The second is the shrubby one, the soporific, and third after these is the manic one. Halicacabum is similar in the power of its leaves to the garden trychnon, but its fruit is diuretic. For this reason it is also mixed into many of the compound drugs that are suited to the liver or bladder or kidneys. Of the soporific strychnon, the bark of the root drunk with wine is soporific when taken in the weight of a drachm. In other respects too it is similar to the juice of the poppy, but weaker to such an extent that it belongs to the third order of cooling things, whereas that is ranked in the fourth. The seed of this trychnon has a diuretic power, but it causes madness when more than twelve clusters are drunk. The remaining one of the trychna mentioned is of no use for the treatment of internal parts. For if four drachms of them are taken, they bring on death, and if fewer, madness. One drachm is harmless, but it too has nothing useful. Applied as a plaster externally to the body, it treats malignant and spreading ulcers. Best for this purpose is the bark of the root, which dries in about the second order, where that order is already being completed and the third is beginning, and cools in the beginning of the second order.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.20.1 [1. On hyacinth.] Of hyacinth the root, which is bulb-like, is of the first order in drying, and in cooling of the second order already nearly completed or even of the third beginning. At any rate it is believed to keep boys beardless for a very long time when applied as a plaster in wine. The fruit cleanses gently and is astringent, and for this reason it too is given to those suffering from jaundice with wine, drying already somewhere about the third order, while with respect to heat and cooling it stands in the middle.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀνήβους, ἐπιπλεῖστον
8.20.1 [1. On hyacinth.] The root of hyacinth, being bulbous, is of the first order in drying, and of the second in cooling, the second being by now about completed, or even of the third at its beginning. At any rate, when applied as a plaster in wine, it is believed to keep boys without pubic hair for a very long time. The fruit is gently cleansing and astringent, and for this reason it too is given in wine to those with jaundice. It dries by now somewhere in the third order, but stands midway between heat and cold.

8.20.4 [4. On henbane.] Henbane: the one with black seed is productive of madness and stupefying. Close to it in power is also the one whose seed is moderately yellow; both are to be avoided as useless and deleterious. 8.20.5 [5. On hyperikon.] Hyperikon (St John's wort) heats and dries, being of fine-particled substance, so as to provoke both the menses and urine. For these purposes one should take the whole fruit, not the seed alone, but together with the leaves; applied as a plaster while green, it is able to bring to a scar other lesions and in particular burns. 8.20.5 [5. On hypericum.] Hypericum heats and dries, being of fine-particled substance, so that it provokes both the menses and urine. For these purposes one must take the whole fruit, not the seed alone; but applied green as a plaster together with the leaves, it is able to bring to a scar both other [lesions] and burns. 8.21.2 [β΄. Περὶ φακοῦ.] Φακὸς ὁ ἐπὶ τῶν τελμάτων ὑγρᾶς καὶ ψυχρᾶς ἐστι κράσεως, ἐκ τῆς δευτέρας που τάξεως ὑπάρχων κατ’ ἄμφω. 8.21.3 [γ΄. Περὶ φαληρίδος.] Φαληρίδος τῆς πόας καὶ ὁ χυλὸς καὶ τὸ σπέρμα καὶ τὰ φύλλα πινόμενα πρὸς τὰ τῆς κύστεως ἀλγήματα πεπίστευται βοηθεῖν, ὡς ἐχούσης τι λεπτομερὲς καὶ θερμόν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.21.2 [2. On phakos (duckweed).] Phakos (duckweed), the kind on the marshes, is of a moist and cold mixture, being of roughly the second order in both respects. 8.21.3 [3. On phaleris.] Of the herb phaleris both the juice and the seed and the leaves, when drunk, are believed to help against the pains of the bladder, as it has in it something fine-particled and hot.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
8.21.2 [2. On the lentil.] The lentil that grows on stagnant pools is of a moist and cold mixture, belonging somewhere about the second order in both respects. 8.21.3 [3. On phalaris.] Of the herb phalaris, the juice, the seed and the leaves, when drunk, are believed to help with pains of the bladder, since the herb has something fine-particled and hot.

Greek (Kühn)
8.21.4 [δ΄. Περὶ φαλαγγίτου.] Φαλαγγίτης. ὠνόμασται μὲν οὕτως ἐκ τοῦ βοηθεῖν τοῖς ὑπὸ φαλαγγίων δακνομένοις. ἔστι δὲ λεπτομεροῦς δυνάμεως ξηραντικῆς, ὅθεν καὶ στροφουμένοις ἀρήγειν δοκεῖ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.21.4 [4. On phalangites.] Phalangites. It has been so named from its helping those bitten by phalangia. It is of a fine-particled, drying power, whence it is also thought to be of help to those suffering from colic.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φαλαγγίων, στροφουμένοις
8.21.4 [4. On phalangitēs.] Phalangitēs. It has been so named from its helping those bitten by phalangia. It is of a fine-particled drying power, whence it is also thought to help those suffering from griping.

Greek (Kühn)
8.21.5 [ε΄. Περὶ φλόμου.] Φλόμος, ἔνιοι δὲ διὰ τοῦ π γράφουσί τε καὶ λέγουσιν. ἡ μέν τίς ἐστι τοῖς φύλλοις λευκὴ, ἡ δὲ μέλαινα. καὶ τῆς λευκῆς ἡ μὲν ἄῤῥην, ἧς τὰ φύλλα στενώτερα, ἡ δὲ θῆλυς, ἧς μείζω. καὶ πρὸς ταύταις ἑτέρα φλόμος ἀγρία προσαγορευομένη, χρυσοειδὴς δ’ ἐστὶ καὶ μηλίζουσα τοῖς ἄνθεσιν. ἄλλη δέ τις ἐπὶ ταύταις, ἣν ἰδίως φλομίδα καὶ θρυαλλίδα προσαγορεύουσι. τῶν μὲν οὖν πρώτων δυοῖν ἡ ῥίζα στρυφνὴ γευομένη οὖσα τοῖς ῥοώδεσι πάθεσιν ἀρήγει. διακλύζονται δ’ αὐτὴν ἔνιοι καὶ πρὸς ὀδόντων ἀλγήματα. τὰ μέντοι φύλλα διαφορητικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως. οὕτω δὲ καὶ τὰ τῶν ἄλλων φύλλα καὶ μάλιστα τῆς χρυσιζούσης τοῖς ἄνθεσιν, ᾗ καὶ τὰς τρίχας ξανθίζουσιν. ἁπασῶν γὰρ τὰ φύλλα ξηραντικῆς τε καὶ μετρίως ῥυπτικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μηλίζουσα
8.21.5 [5. On phlomos.] Phlomos (mullein) — some people both write and pronounce it with a p. One kind is white in its leaves, the other black. And of the white kind one is the male, whose leaves are narrower, the other the female, whose leaves are larger. And in addition to these there is another phlomos, called wild, which is golden and quince-yellow in its flowers. And yet another besides these, which they specifically call phlomis and thryallis. Now the root of the first two, being acerbic to the taste, helps in conditions of flux. Some people also rinse the mouth with it for pains of the teeth. The leaves, however, are of dispersing power. So too are the leaves of the others, and especially those of the one that is golden in its flowers, with which they also make the hair yellow. For the leaves of all of them are of a drying and moderately cleansing power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μηλίζουσα, θρυαλλίδα, ῥοώδεσι, διακλύζονται
8.21.5 [5. On mullein (phlomos).] Phlomos: some people both write and pronounce it with a π. One kind is white in its leaves, the other black. Of the white, one is male, whose leaves are narrower, the other female, whose leaves are larger. Besides these there is another phlomos called wild, which is gold-like and yellowish (μηλίζουσα) in its flowers. And besides these there is yet another, which they call specifically phlomis and thryallis. Now the root of the first two, being acerbic to the taste, helps fluxional affections. Some also rinse the mouth with it for pains of the teeth. The leaves, however, are of a dispersing power. So too are the leaves of the others, and especially of the one that is golden in its flowers, with which they also dye the hair blond. For the leaves of all of them are of a drying and moderately cleansing power.

Greek (Kühn)
8.21.6 [στ΄. Περὶ φοίνικος.] Φοῖνιξ τὸ δένδρον στυπτικῆς μετέχει δυνάμεως ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς ἑαυτοῦ μέρεσιν. ὁ μὲν οὖν τῶν κλάδων χυλὸς αὐστηρός ἐστιν ἐξ ὑδατώδους οὐσίας χλιαρᾶς καὶ γεώδους ψυχρᾶς συγκείμενος. ὁμοίας δὲ τούτου· φύσεώς ἐστι καὶ ὁ καλούμενος ἐγκέφαλος ἐδώδιμος ὑπάρχων. ὁ δὲ καρπὸς αὐτοῦ καὶ μάλισθ’ ὁ γλυκὺς οὐκ ὀλίγης μετείληφε θερμότητος. ἐδώδιμος δ’ ὢν εἰς πολλὴν ἔρχεται χρῆσιν, οὐ μόνον ἔξωθεν ἐπιτιθέμενος, ἵνα τονῶσαί τι καὶ ξηρᾶναι καὶ συναγαγεῖν καὶ πιλῆσαι καὶ πυκνῶσαι χρῄζομεν, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὡς σιτίον λαμβανόμενος ἅμα τε τοῖς ἄλλοις καὶ αὐτὸς καθ’ ἑαυτόν. ὃ δὲ καλοῦσιν ἐλάτην, τὸ ἁπαλὸν ἐκβλάστημα τοῦ φοίνικος, τὴν αὐτὴν τῷ κατ’ αὐτὴν ἐγκεφάλῳ δύναμιν ἔχει. τὸ δ’ οἷον ἀμφίεσμα αὐτοῦ στυπτικὴν μὲν καὶ αὐτὸ κέκτηται ποιότητα, ξηραίνει δ’ ἐπὶ πλέον ἁπάντων τῶν εἰρημένων, ὡς ἂν καὶ τῇ τῆς οὐσίας συστάσει ξηρότερον αὐτῶν ὑπάρχον καὶ ἥκιστα μετέχον ὑγρότητος, ὥστε δεόντως ἐπὶ τῶν σηπεδονωδῶν ἑλκῶν αὐτῷ χρῶνται. καὶ τοῖς συνάγουσι τὰ κεχαλασμένα τῶν ἄρθρων φαρμάκοις μιγνύουσι καὶ ταῖς ἡπατικαῖς τε καὶ στομαχικαῖς δυνάμεσιν, ἔξωθέν τε καὶ ἔσωθεν. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἡ ῥίζα τοῦ φυτοῦ ξηραντικῆς ἀδήκτως δυνάμεως, ἔχουσά τι καὶ αὐτὴ στύψεως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐλάτην, δυνάμεσιν
8.21.6 [6. On the date palm.] The date palm, the tree, partakes of astringent power in all its parts. Thus the juice of the branches is harsh, being composed of a watery substance that is lukewarm and an earthy substance that is cold. Of a similar nature to this is also what is called the "brain," which is edible. Its fruit, and especially the sweet kind, has partaken of no small heat. Being edible, it comes into much use, not only when applied externally, where we need to tone something and to dry and to draw together and to compact and to condense, but also when taken as food, both together with other things and by itself. What they call elate, the tender shoot of the date palm, has the same power as the brain within it. Its covering, as it were, also itself possesses an astringent quality, but it dries more than all the things mentioned, inasmuch as in the consistency of its substance it is drier than they and partakes least of moisture, so that they rightly use it on putrefactive ulcers. And they mix it into the drugs that draw together relaxed joints and into the hepatic and stomachic preparations, both external and internal. The root of the plant too is of a drying power without biting, having itself also something of astringency.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὁμοίας δὲ τούτου· φύσεώς (punctuation in Kühn's text is odd; construed as ὁμοίας δὲ τούτου φύσεως), τῷ κατ’ αὐτὴν ἐγκεφάλῳ (the feminine αὐτήν is awkward; possibly to be read κατ’ αὐτόν; rendered 'the brain within it'), ἐλάτην, ἀμφίεσμα, πιλῆσαι, δυνάμεσιν (rendered as 'remedies', in the sense of compound medicines), ἐγκέφαλος (the palm 'brain', i.e. the edible pith)
8.21.6 [6. On the date palm.] The date palm, the tree, has a share of astringent power in all of its parts. Now the juice of the branches is harsh, being composed of a lukewarm watery substance and a cold earthy one. Of a nature similar to this is also the so-called 'brain', which is edible. Its fruit, and especially the sweet kind, has taken on no small heat. Being edible, it comes into wide use. It is not only applied externally, where we need to tone, dry, draw together, compact and condense something, but it is also taken as food, both together with other things and by itself. What they call elatē, the tender shoot of the palm, has the same power as the brain within it. Its so-to-speak wrapping also possesses an astringent quality, but it dries more than all the things mentioned, since it is drier than they are in the consistency of its substance as well and has the least share of moisture. So they rightly use it for putrefactive ulcers. They also mix it into the drugs that draw together the relaxed parts of the joints, and into hepatic and stomachic remedies, both external and internal. The root of the plant, too, is of a drying power without biting, and it also has something of astringency itself.

    What they call elatē, the tender shoot of the palm, has the same power as the brain within it.

Περὶ χαμαιλέοντος.] Χαμαιλέοντος ἡ ῥίζα τοῦ μὲν μέλανος, ἔχει τι δηλητήριον, ὅθεν ἔξωθεν ἡ χρῆσις αὐτοῦ πρὸς ψώρας καὶ λειχῆνας καὶ ἀλφοὺς καὶ ὅλως ὅσα ῥύψεως δεῖται καὶ τοῖς μαλακτικοῖς καὶ διαφορητικοῖς μίγνυται φαρμάκοις. καὶ τὰ φαγεδαινικὰ τῶν ἑλκῶν ἰᾶται καταπλασθεῖσα. ἔστι γὰρ ξηρὰ κατὰ τὴν τρίτην ἤδη που τάξιν. ἐν δὲ τῷ θερμαίνειν ἐκ τῆς δευτέρας συμπληρουμένης. ἡ δὲ τοῦ λευκοῦ χαμαιλέοντος ῥίζα πρὸς ἕλμινθας πλατείας δίδοται πλῆ θος ὀξυβάφου μετ’ οἴνου αὐστηροῦ. προσφέρουσι δ’ αὐτὴν καὶ τοῖς ὑδερικοῖς παραπλησίαν οὖσαν καὶ αὐτὴν τῇ κράσει τῇ τοῦ μέλανος ῥίζῃ, πλὴν ὅσα πικροτέρα ἐκείνης ἐστίν.

8.22.5 [5. On chamaileuke.] Chamaileuke is hot in power at about the third rank and dry at the first. 8.22.6 [6. On chamaileon.] The root of chamaileon, that of the black kind, has something poisonous in it, whence its use is external, against psorai and lichens and alphoi and in general whatever needs cleansing, and it is mixed with softening and dispersing drugs. And it heals the phagedaenic ulcers when applied as a plaster. For it is dry at about the third rank already, but in heating it is of the second rank at its completion. The root of the white chamaileon is given against flat worms, in the quantity of an oxybaphon, with harsh wine. They administer it also to the dropsical, since it too is similar in mixture to the root of the black kind, except insofar as it is more bitter than that one.

8.22.5 [5. On chamaeleuce.] Chamaeleuce is hot in its power somewhere about the third order, and dry in the first. 8.22.6 [6. On chamaeleon.] The root of the black chamaeleon has something poisonous in it, so its use is external, for psorai and leichenes and alphoi and in general whatever needs cleansing; and it is mixed into softening and dispersing drugs. Applied as a poultice, it also heals the phagedaenic ones among ulcers. For it is dry already somewhere about the third order, and in heating it is of the second order at its completion. The root of the white chamaeleon is given for flat worms, in the amount of an oxybaphon, with harsh wine. 8.22.7 [7. On chamaepitys.] Chamaepitys, in respect of taste, has the bitter flavour stronger than the pungent, while in respect of its action it thoroughly purges and cleanses the viscera more than it heats. For this reason it is a good drug both for those suffering from jaundice and in general for those whose liver is readily obstructed. It also draws down the menses, both when drunk and when applied as a pessary with honey. It is also a diuretic drug. Some people also give it to those suffering from sciatica, boiling it down in honey-water (melikraton). When green, the herb is also able to glue together even large wounds, and it heals putrefactive ulcers, and it disperses the hardnesses in the breasts. For in drying it belongs to the third order, and in heating to the second.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προστιθεμένη
8.22.7 [7. On chamaepitys.] Chamaepitys, in taste, has the bitter flavour stronger than the pungent; in its activity, however, it purges and cleanses the viscera more than it heats. For this reason it is a good drug for the icteric and, in general, for those whose liver is easily obstructed. It also brings on the menses, both when drunk and when applied with honey. It is also a diuretic drug. Some people also give it to sciatics, boiling it down in honey-water. The herb when green is able to conglutinate even large wounds, heals putrefactive ulcers, and disperses hardnesses in the breasts. For it is of the third order in drying, and of the second in heating.

8.22.9 [θ΄. Περὶ χελιδονίου.] Χελιδόνιον ῥυπτικῆς ἰσχυρῶς ἐστι καὶ θερμῆς δυνάμεως· καὶ μέντοι καὶ ὁ χυλὸς αὐτῆς εἰς ὀξυωπίας ἐστὶ χρήσιμος ἐφ’ ὧν ἀθροίζεται παχύ τι κατὰ τὴν κόρην, διαψορεῖσθαι δεόμενον. ἐχρήσαντο δ’ ἔνιοι τῇ ῥίζῃ καὶ πρὸς τοὺς ὑπ’ ἐμφράξεως ἥπατος ἰκτεριῶντας, ἐν οἴνῳ λευκῷ διδόντες πίνειν σὺν ἀνίσῳ. διαμασωμένη χρήσιμός ἐστιν ὀδόντων ἀλγήμασι. τὸ δὲ μικρότερον χελιδόνιον, δριμύτερον ὑπάρχον τοῦ μείζονος, ἑλκοῖ ταχέως τὸ δέρμα καταπλασσόμενον, ὄνυχάς τε λεπροὺς ἐκβάλλει. καθαίρει δ’ ὁ χυλὸς αὐτοῦ διὰ ῥινῶν ἰσχυρῶς ὑπάρχων δριμὺς, ὥστε εἴη ἂν καὶ ἥδε ἡ πόα τῆς τετάρτης ἤδη που τάξεως συμπληρουμένης ἤδη καὶ κατὰ τὸ θερμαίνειν καὶ ξηραίνειν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: διαψορεῖσθαι, συμπληρουμένης
8.22.9 [9. On chelidonion.] Chelidonion is of a strongly cleansing and hot power; and indeed its juice too is useful for sharpness of sight in those cases in which something thick collects at the pupil and needs to be dispersed. Some people have also used the root for those suffering from jaundice as a result of an obstruction of the liver, giving it to drink in white wine with anise. Chewed, it is useful for pains of the teeth. The smaller chelidonion, being more pungent than the greater, quickly ulcerates the skin when applied as a plaster, and casts out scaly (leprous) nails. Its juice purges through the nostrils, being strongly pungent, so that this herb too would belong by now, more or less, to the fourth order, as that order is already being completed, both in respect of heating and of drying.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διαψορεῖσθαι (apparently a corruption of διαφορεῖσθαι; rendered 'be dispersed'), αὐτῆς (feminine, although χελιδόνιον is neuter; taken as 'its', referring to the plant), ὀξυωπίας (rendered 'sharpness of sight'), λεπροὺς (rendered 'leprous', i.e. scaly or rough nails), τῆς τετάρτης ἤδη που τάξεως συμπληρουμένης ἤδη (sense of συμπληρουμένης and the doubled ἤδη uncertain; rendered as the fourth order being by now brought to completion)
8.22.9 [9. On chelidonium.] Chelidonium is of a strongly cleansing and hot power; and indeed its juice too is useful for sharpness of sight in those in whom something thick collects at the pupil that needs to be dispersed. Some people have also used the root for those who are icteric from obstruction of the liver, giving it to drink in white wine with anise. Chewed, it is useful for pains of the teeth. The smaller chelidonium, being more pungent than the larger, quickly ulcerates the skin when applied as a plaster, and it casts off leprous nails. Its juice, being strongly pungent, purges through the nostrils, so that this herb too would belong somewhere to the fourth order, the order being by now brought to completion, both in heating and in drying.

8.22.11 [ια΄. Περὶ χόνδρου.] Χόνδρος ἐστὶ μὲν δήπου καὶ τροφὴ βελτίστη, καθάπερ καὶ πυρὸς, ἀλλ’ οὐ τῆς νῦν πραγματείας ἐστὶ περὶ τροφῆς διαιρεῖσθαι, ὡς φάρμακον δὲ τῆς ἐμπλαστικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως. ἐν δὲ τῇ κράσει παραπλήσιός ἐστι πυρῷ, πλέον ἐκείνου τὸ γλίσχρον ἔχων, ᾧ καὶ τρόφιμος αὐτοῦ μᾶλλόν ἐστι καὶ οἷον ὕλη τις εἰς ὑποδοχὴν ἐπιτήδειος ὄξους τε γίγνεται καὶ θαλάττης καὶ ἅλμης, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα σφοδρῶς ξηραίνοντα· καὶ διὰ τοῦτό τινες ἐκ τῶν ἱκανῶς ξηραινόντων ἐνόμισαν εἶναι καὶ τοῦτο τὸ σπέρμα. ἔστι δ’ αὐτὸς ὁ χόνδρος ἐκ τῶν συμπέττειν δυναμένων μᾶλλον, ὥσπερ καὶ ὁ πυρὸς, ἅπερ ἥκιστα ξηραίνει. διὰ δὲ τὴν τῶν ξηραινόντων φαρμάκων μίξιν τὸ σύνθετον ἐξ αὐτῶν ξηραντικὸν γινόμενον ἐξαπατᾷ τοὺς πολλοὺς τῶν ἰατρῶν, ὡς αὐτῷ τῷ χόνδρῳ τὴν τοῦ ξηραίνειν αἰτίαν ἀνατιθέναι παρέντας τὰ μιγνύμενα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
8.22.11 [11. On chondros.] Chondros is, to be sure, also an excellent food, just as wheat is too, but it is not the business of the present treatise to draw distinctions concerning food; as a drug, however, it is of clogging power. In its mixture it is similar to wheat, having more of the viscous quality than that, by which it is both more nourishing than it and becomes, as it were, a kind of material suitable for the reception of vinegar, sea-water and brine, and whatever other such things dry vigorously; and for this reason some have supposed that this seed too is among the things that dry sufficiently. But chondros itself belongs rather among the things capable of concocting, just as wheat does, and these dry least of all. It is because of the admixture of drying drugs that the compound made from them, becoming drying, deceives most physicians, so that they attribute the cause of the drying to chondros itself, disregarding the things mixed in.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χόνδρος, συμπέττειν
8.22.11 [11. On groats.] Groats (chondros) are, of course, also a most excellent food, as wheat is too; but it is not the business of the present treatise to draw distinctions about food. As a drug, groats are of the clogging power. In their mixture they are close to wheat, having more of the viscous than it does, and by this they are also more nourishing than it, and they become a kind of material suitable for the reception of vinegar, sea-water and brine, and of such other things as dry strongly. For this reason some have thought that this seed too is among those that dry considerably. But groats themselves are rather among the things able to assist concoction, as wheat is too, and these dry least of all. Because of the admixture of drying drugs, however, the compound made from them becomes drying and deceives most physicians, so that they attribute the cause of the drying to the groats themselves and disregard the things mixed in.

8.22.12 [ιβ΄. Περὶ χρυσοκόμης.] Χρυσοκόμη, καλεῖται δὲ καὶ χρυσίτης· τὴν δριμεῖαν ἅμα καὶ στύφουσαν ἐπικρατούσας ἔχει ποιότητας ἡ ῥίζα, διὸ μηδὲ πολύχρηστός ἐστιν. ἥ γε μὴν χρῆσις αὐτῆς ἐν μελικράτῳ καθεψηθείσης ἐν περιπνευμονίαις τε καὶ ἡπατικοῖς νοσήμασιν. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ τῆς ἐμμήνου καθάρσεως προτρεπτικόν.

8.22.12 [12. On chrysokome.] Chrysokome, which is also called chrysites: its root has the pungent together with the astringent quality as the predominant ones, for which reason it is not of manifold use either. Its use, at any rate, when it has been boiled down in honey-water (melikraton), is in cases of peripneumonia and in hepatic diseases. It also has something that promotes the purgation of the menses.

8.22.12 [12. On chrysocome.] Chrysocome, which is also called chrysites: its root has as its predominant qualities the pungent and at the same time the astringent, and for this reason it is not of many uses. Its use, however, when it has been boiled down in honey-water, is in peripneumoniae and in hepatic diseases. It also has something that promotes the purgation of the menses.

8.24.2 [β΄. Περὶ ὠκιμοειδοῦς.] Ὠκιμοειδές· ἔνιοι δὲ φιλεταίριον ὀνομάζουσιν· ἄχρηστος μὲν ἡ ῥίζα, τὸ δὲ σπέρμα λεπτομεροῦς τε καὶ ξηραντικῆς ἀδήκτως ὑπάρχει δυνάμεως. ΓΑΛΗΝΟΥ ΠΕΡΙ ΤΗς ΤΩΝ ΑΠΛΩΝ ΦΑΡΜΑΚΩΝ ΚΡΑΣΕΩς ΚΑΙ ΔΥΝΑΜΕΩς ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ Ι. Προοίμιον. Ὅσα μὲν τῶν φυτῶν ἐστι μόρια καὶ καρποὶ καὶ χυλοὶ καὶ ὀποὶ πρόσθεν εἴρηται· νυνὶ δὲ τῶν ὑπολοίπων φαρμάκων ὅσα μεταλλεύεται καὶ ὅσα τῆς γῆς αὐτῆς ἐστιν εἴδη πρόκειται διελθεῖν. ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐτῷ εἰρήσεταί τι καὶ περὶ τῶν ἐν τοῖς ζώοις μορίων, οἷς ἐν λόγῳ φαρμάκων χρώμεθα πρὸς τὰς ἰάσεις. κοινὸν δὲ τινα περὶ πάντων αὐτῶν λόγον ἄμεινον εἶναί μοι δοκεῖ προτάξαι σαφηνείας τε ἅμα καὶ τοῦ διηρθρωμένως ἀκούειν ἕνεκα τῶν εἰρημένων. ἐὰν γὰρ ἕποιτό τις τὰ πάντα τοῖς γεγραφόσιν ἤτοι τὰς περὶ τῶν φαρμάκων πραγματείας ἢ τὰς περὶ ὕλης ἢ τὰς περὶ σκευασίας αὐτῶν, ἐν πολλοῖς σφαλήσεται μέγιστα καὶ παρακούσει τῶν ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ διωρισμένως λεγομένων. δυοῖν δ’ ὄντων κεφαλαίων τοῦ μέλλοντος λεχθήσεσθαι λόγου κοινοῦ, τὸ μὲν ἕτερόν ἐστιν εἰ τῶν αὐτοφυῶν φαρμάκων τὰ κεκαυμένα θερμότερα χρὴ νομίζειν ἢ ψυχρότερα, τὸ δ’ ἕτερον ὑπὲρ τῶν στυφόντων φαρμάκων, ὧν ἐν εἴδει πρόσθεν ἐδείχθη τὰ αὐστηρὰ καὶ τὰ στρυφνά. λέλεκται μὲν οὖν ἤδη περὶ τούτων ἐν τῷ τετάρτῳ τῶνδε τῶν βιβλίων, ἀναμνῆσαι δὲ καὶ νῦν ἄμεινον ὡς ἐναντιωτάτη φαίνεται ποιότης καὶ δύναμις εἶναι τοῖς στύφουσι φαρμάκοις πρὸς τὰ δριμέα. στύφει μὲν γὰρ ἀκακία καὶ βαλαύστιον, ὑπόκυστίς τε καὶ κύτινοι καὶ κηκὶς καὶ ῥῆον καὶ ῥοῦς, ὀμφάκιόν τε καὶ μέσπιλα, καὶ κρανία καὶ ῥοιᾶς λέμματα καὶ μύρτα. δριμέα δ’ ἐστὶν εὐφόρβιον καὶ σκόροδα καὶ κρόμμυα καὶ πράσα καὶ νᾶπυ καὶ πέπερι καὶ γιγγίβερι καὶ σμύρνιον, ὀρίγανόν τε καὶ γλήχων, καὶ καλαμίνθη καὶ θύμος. χρὴ τοίνυν ἀναμνησθῆναι μόνον ἡμᾶς ὁποίαν αἴσθησιν ἔχομεν ἑκάστου τῶν εἰρημένων. ἀκολουθήσει γὰρ εὐθέως ἡ διαφορὰ τῆς ποιότητος αὐτῶν, ἣν ἐν τῷ τετάρτῳ λόγῳ διῆλθον ἅμα ταῖς ἄλλαις γευσταῖς ἁπάσαις διαφοραῖς. συνάγει μὲν οὖν καὶ σφίγγει καὶ πιλεῖ τὴν οὐσίαν ἡμῶν τὰ στύφοντα, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἐπιτιθέμενα, καθ’ ὅ τι ἂν ἐθελήσῃς μέλος ἔξωθεν, εὐθέως ἀποδείκνυσιν αὐτὸ ῥυσόν τε καὶ προσεσταλμένον. ἔμπαλιν δὲ τούτοις τὰ δριμέα κατὰ τοῦ δέρματος ἐπιτιθέμενα θερμαίνει τε σαφῶς αὐτὸ καὶ εἰς ὄγκον συναίρει σὺν ἐρυθρῷ χρώματι, καὶ εἰ χρονίσειεν, ἑλκοῖ. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἐναργῶς φαίνεται τέμνοντά τε καὶ θερμαίνοντα, καὶ διὰ τοῦθ’ ἕλκοντα πρὸς ἑαυτὰ τὸ ἐκ τῶν πλησιαζόντων μορίων αἷμα. τὰ στύφοντα δ’ ἀποκρουόμενα τὸ περιεχόμενον ἐν αὐτοῖς τῷ ψύχειν τε καὶ συνάγειν καὶ πιλεῖν πέφυκεν. ἡ δύναμίς τε οὖν ἐναντιωτάτη τοῖς στύφουσίν ἐστι πρὸς τὰ δριμέα, καὶ ἡ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν ποιότης οὐδὲν ὅμοιον ἔχουσα. πῶς οὖν ἔνιοι καὶ τὸ πέπερι καὶ τὰ σκόροδα καὶ πάντα τὰ δριμέα στύφειν λέγουσιν οὐδ’ ἐπινοῆσαι δυνατόν. εἰ μὲν γὰρ ὥσπερ ταῦτα στύφειν, οὕτω καὶ ῥοῦν καὶ βαλαύστιον ὀμφάκιόν τε καὶ μέσπιλον ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα δριμέα προσηγόρευον, ὑπαλλάττοντες τὸ τῶν Ἑλλήνων ἔθος ἐν τοῖς ὀνόμασιν, ἀγνοεῖν μὲν ἂν αὐτοὺς ὑπολάβοιμεν τὰς φωνὰς τῶν Ἑλλήνων ἅμα τοῖς ὑπ’ αὐτῶν σημαινομένοις, οὐ μὴν ἀναισθήτους γε κατὰ τὴν γευστικὴν εἶναι δύναμιν, ἢ τὴν ὀσφρητικήν· ἐπεὶ δ’ ἑνὶ προσαγορεύουσιν ὀνόματι πράγματα δύο, καὶ τῇ κατὰ τὴν ὄσφρησιν αἰσθήσει καὶ τῇ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν, οἷς τε φαίνονται πράττοντα φύσιν ἐναντιωτάτην ἀλλήλοις ἔχοντα, θαυμάσαι προσήκει τοὺς ἀνθρώπους ἢ ἕνεκεν τῆς ἀναισθησίας ἤ τῆς ἀνοίας ἢ καὶ ἀμφοτέρου ἅμα. παραπλήσιον γάρ τοι ποιοῦσιν τῷ λέγοντι τὴν χιόνα τῷ πυρὶ τὴν αὐτὴν αἴσθησιν ἐργάζεσθαι, καί τις ὑπὸ συνηθείας τῆς εἰς τοσοῦτον ἀλλοκότου χρήσεως τῶν ὀνομάτων, ἔφη μοί ποτε μηδὲν κωλύειν φάναι τὴν αὐτὴν ἔχειν ποιότητά τε καὶ δύναμιν τῷ πυρὶ τὴν χιόνα· καὶ γὰρ καὶ ταύτην ὦφθαι πολλάκις ἀποκαίουσαν τοὺς πόδας τῶν δι’ αὐτῆς ἐπιπολὺ βαδισάντων· τῶν μὲν δὴ τοιούτων ἀνθρώπων οὐ σμικροῦ χρόνου χρεία τὸν ῥύπον ἀποκαθῆραι τῆς ψυχῆς. ὅσοι δ’ οὐχ οὕτως ἠτύχησαν ὡς ἐν ἀμαθίᾳ τελέᾳ διαβιῶναι, προανεγνωκόσι τὸ τέταρτον τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων, ἀρκεῖ τούτοις ἀναμνήσεως ἕνεκα τὰ μέχρι δεῦρο λελεγμένα, χάριν τοῦ διηρθρωμένως ἀκούειν τῶν ὀνομάτων ἐφ’ ἑκάστου τῶν οἰκείων πραγμάτων, ὡς ἅπαντες Ἕλληνες εἰώθασιν χρῆσθαι. μεταβήσομαι δ’ ἐπὶ τὸ δεύτερον ἤδη σκέμμα, μηκέθ’ ὑπὲρ ὀνόματός τε καὶ τοῦ κατ’ αὐτὸ σημαινομένου γιγνόμενον, ἀλλὰ περὶ φύσεως πράγματος. οἱ μὲν γὰρ πλεῖστοι νομίζουσι τὰ καυθέντα πάντα ψυχρότερα γίγνεσθαι σφῶν αὐτῶν, ἔνιοι δ’ ἔμπαλιν αὐξάνεσθαι τὴν θερμασίαν οἴονται τῶν καυθέντων ἁπάντων, ἁμαρτάνοντες ἑκάτεροι. φανίονται γὰρ ἐναργῶς ἔνια μὲν θερμότερα γινόμενα, κατά τε τὴν γεῦσιν καὶ τὴν ἁφὴν καὶ τὴν ἐν τῇ χρήσει θεωρουμένην δύναμιν, ὡς ἔμπροσθεν εἶπον ἐπί τε τῶν δριμέων καὶ τῶν στυφόντων, ἔνια δ’ ἔμπαλιν ἧττον θερμὰ φαινόμενα μετὰ τὸ καυθῆναι· καὶ τοῦτο διαγινώσκομεν σαφῶς τῇ τε αἰσθήσει καὶ τῇ χρήσει. λέγω δὲ χρῆσιν, ﻿ ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τῶν ἔμπροσθεν εἶπον, ὅταν ἐπιτιθέντα τῷ δέρματι τὰ μὲν ἐρυθρότερά τε καὶ θερμότερα αὐτὰ ποιῇ, τὰ δὲ ἄναιμά τε καὶ ψυχρὰ, καὶ τὰ μὲν εἰς ὄγκον ἐξαίρῃ, τὰ δὲ προστέλλῃ. τὰ μὲν οὖν δριμέα πολὺ τῆς θερμότητος ἀπόλλυσι καυθέντα, τὰ δὲ μὴ τοιαῦτα προσλαμβάνει, τελέως δὲ ψυχρὸν οὐδὲν τῶν καυθέντων ἐστίν. ἐγκαταλείπεται γὰρ αὐτοῖς οἷον ἐμπύρευμά τι· καὶ γὰρ προσηγόρευεν οὕτως Ἀριστοτέλης αὐτὸ, καὶ τοῦτ’ ἔστι τὸ κατὰ τὰς πλύσεις ἀποῤῥυπτόμενον. ἔστι δὲ τὸ λεπτομερέστατον τῆς τῶν καυθέντων οὐσίας, οὗ συναπελθόντος τῷ ὕδατι τὸ λοιπὸν τοῦ καυθέντος οὐσία γεώδης ἐστί. τὸ μὲν γὰρ ὑγρὸν ἅπαν ἡ καῦσις ἐκδαπανᾷ, τὸ δ’ ὑπολειπόμενον γεῶδές ἐστιν ἅμα τῷ πρὸς Ἀριστοτέλους ἐμπυρεύματι κληθέντι. τοῦτ’ οὖν ὅταν τις ἀφέληται καὶ χωρίσῃ τῇ πλύσει, τὸ μὲν ὕδωρ, ᾧ τὸ φάρμακον ἐπλύθη, θερμὴν δύναμιν ἐπεκτήσατο λεπτομερῆ, τὸ δ’ ὑπόλοιπον γίνεται γεῶδες ψυχρὸν, ξηραίνειν ἀδήκτως δυνάμενον. εἴρηται μὲν οὖν μοι καὶ περὶ τούτων ἔμπροσθεν, ἀλλ’ οὐδὲν χεῖρον ἀναμνῆσαι καὶ νῦν, ἵνα τις ὑπόγυον ἐσχηκὼς τὴν ἀνάμνησιν αὐτῶν ἕπηται τοῖς λεχθησομένοις ἀκριβέστερον.
