8.24.2 [2. On okimoeides.] Okimoeides—some call it philetairion—: the root is useless, but the seed is of a fine-particled and drying power, without biting. GALEN, ON THE MIXTURE AND POWER OF SIMPLE DRUGS, BOOK 9. Proem. Those drugs that are parts of plants, and fruits, and juices, and saps have been spoken of before; now it is proposed to go through, of the remaining drugs, those that are mined and those that are kinds of earth itself. Following upon this, something will also be said about the parts in animals which we use in the capacity of drugs for cures. It seems to me better to set out first a certain general account concerning all of them, both for the sake of clarity and so that what has been said may be heard in an articulated manner. For if anyone were to follow in all things those who have written either treatises on drugs or on materia medica or on their preparation, he will go greatly wrong in many matters and will misunderstand what is said by me with precise distinction. The general account that is about to be given has two chief heads: the one is whether, among self-grown drugs, those that have been burnt should be considered hotter or colder; the other concerns astringent drugs, of which the harsh and the acerbic were earlier shown to be species. Now these matters have already been discussed in the fourth of these books, but it is better to recall now too that the quality and power of astringent drugs appears to be most opposite to pungent ones. For acacia is astringent, and balaustion, and hypokistis and kytinoi and oak-gall and rheon and sumach, and omphakion and medlars, and cornels and pomegranate rinds and myrtle-berries. Pungent are euphorbion and garlic and onions and leeks and mustard and pepper and ginger and smyrnion, and origanon and pennyroyal, and calamint and thyme. We need, then, only recall what sort of sensation we have of each of the things mentioned. For the difference of their quality will follow immediately—the difference which I went through in the fourth book together with all the other gustatory differences. Astringents, then, contract and bind and compact our substance, and for this reason, when applied externally to whatever part you wish, they immediately show it wrinkled and drawn in. Conversely to these, pungent things applied to the skin clearly heat it and raise it into a swelling together with a red colour, and if they remain long, they ulcerate it. These, then, manifestly appear to cut and to heat, and for this reason to draw towards themselves the blood from the neighbouring parts. Astringents, on the other hand, are of a nature to repel what is contained within them by cooling and contracting and compacting. The power of astringents, then, is most opposite to pungent things, and the quality according to taste has nothing similar. How, then, some say that both pepper and garlic and all pungent things are astringent, it is not even possible to conceive. For if, just as they say these are astringent, so they also called sumach and balaustion and omphakion and medlar and all other such things pungent, altering the usage of the Greeks in their names, we would suppose them ignorant of the words of the Greeks together with the things signified by them, but not, at any rate, insensible with respect to the gustatory faculty or the olfactory; but since they call by one name two things which, both in the sensation of smell and in that of taste, and in what they are seen to do, have a nature most opposite to one another, it is fitting to marvel at the men, whether for their insensibility or for their folly or for both at once. For they do something very like the man who says that snow produces the same sensation as fire; and indeed someone, out of habituation to so bizarre a use of names, once told me that nothing prevented one from saying that snow has the same quality and power as fire; for it too has often been seen to burn off the feet of those who have walked through it for a long way. For such men, then, no short time is needed to cleanse away the filth from the soul. But for those who have not been so unfortunate as to live out their lives in complete ignorance, having read beforehand the fourth of these treatises, what has been said up to this point suffices by way of reminder, for the sake of hearing in an articulated manner the names as applied to each of the things proper to them, as all Greeks are accustomed to use them. I shall now pass on to the second inquiry, which is no longer about a name and what is signified by it, but about the nature of a thing. For most people believe that all things that have been burnt become colder than they themselves were, while some, on the contrary, think that the heat of all burnt things is increased—both sides being in error. For some things manifestly appear to become hotter, both to taste and to touch and in the power observed in use, as I said earlier regarding pungent and astringent things, while others on the contrary appear less hot after being burnt; and this we recognize clearly both by sensation and by use. I mean by 'use', just as I said regarding the earlier cases, when things applied to the skin make it in some cases redder and hotter, in others bloodless and cold, and some raise it into a swelling, while others draw it in. Pungent things, then, lose much of their heat when burnt, while those not of this kind acquire heat; but none of the things burnt is completely cold. For there is left behind in them a kind of empyreuma, as it were; for so Aristotle named it, and this is what is washed off in washings. It is the most fine-particled part of the substance of burnt things, and when this has gone off together with the water, the remainder of the burnt thing is an earthy substance. For the burning consumes all the moisture, and what is left behind is earthy, together with what was called by Aristotle the empyreuma. When, then, someone takes this away and separates it by washing, the water in which the drug was washed acquires a fine-particled hot power, while the remainder becomes earthy and cold, able to dry without biting. I have spoken about these matters before as well, but it is no worse to recall them now too, so that anyone who has a fresh recollection of them may follow more accurately what is to be said.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ Ι (the numeral Ι = 10 conflicts with the start of Book 9; possibly corrupt), ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐτῷ, κύτινοι, ῥῆον, ὀμφάκιον, ἐμπύρευμα, ἀποκαίουσαν, καὶ τῇ κατὰ τὴν ὄσφρησιν αἰσθήσει καὶ τῇ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν, οἷς τε φαίνονται πράττοντα (construction loose), ὕλης
8.24.2 [2. On ōkimoeides.] Ōkimoeides: some call it philetairion. Its root is useless, but its seed is of a fine-particled and drying power, without biting. GALEN, ON THE MIXTURE AND POWER OF SIMPLE DRUGS, BOOK X. Preface. Those drugs that are parts of plants, and fruits, and juices, and saps, have been discussed before. It is now proposed to go through the remaining drugs: those that are mined, and those that are kinds of earth itself. After this something will also be said about the parts in animals that we use as drugs for cures. It seems better to me, however, to set first a common account about all of them, for the sake both of clarity and of hearing what is said in an articulated way. For if someone were to follow in everything those who have written treatises either on drugs, or on materia, or on their preparation, he will err very greatly in many things and will mishear what I say with distinctions. The common account about to be given has two heads. The one is whether the burnt among the self-grown drugs should be considered hotter or colder. The other concerns the astringent drugs, of which the harsh and the acerbic were shown earlier to be species. These matters have already been discussed in the fourth of these books, but it is better to recall now too that the quality and power of the astringent drugs appears to be most contrary to that of the pungent ones. For acacia and balaustion are astringent, as are hypocistis and kytinoi and oak-gall and rhēon and sumach, omphakion and medlars, and cornels and pomegranate rinds and myrtle-berries. Pungent are euphorbium and garlic and onions and leeks and mustard and pepper and ginger and smyrnion, oregano and pennyroyal, and calamint and thyme. We need, then, only to recall what kind of sensation we have of each of the things mentioned. For the difference of their quality will follow at once, which I went through in the fourth book together with all the other differences of taste. Astringents draw together, constrict and compress our substance. For this reason, when applied externally to whatever limb you please, they at once render it wrinkled and contracted. Conversely, pungents applied to the skin plainly heat it and raise it into a swelling with a red colour, and if they remain long, they ulcerate it. These things plainly appear to be cutting and heating, and for this reason to draw to themselves the blood from the neighbouring parts. Astringents, by contrast, are by nature such as to repel what is contained in the parts, by cooling, drawing together and compressing. So the power of astringents is most contrary to that of pungents, and their quality in taste has nothing similar. How, then, some say that pepper and garlic and all the pungents are astringent, it is not even possible to conceive. Suppose that, just as they call these astringent, they likewise called sumach and balaustion and omphakion and medlar and the other such things pungent, altering the usage of the Greeks in names. In that case we would suppose them ignorant of the words of the Greeks together with what is signified by them, but not insensible in their gustatory or olfactory faculty. But since they call by one name two things which have a nature most contrary to one another, both to the sense of smell and to that of taste, and in what they are seen to do, it is fitting to wonder at these people, either for their insensibility, or for their folly, or for both at once. For they do something very like a man who says that snow produces the same sensation as fire. And indeed someone, from habituation to so outlandish a use of names, once told me that nothing prevents saying that snow has the same quality and power as fire, for snow too has often been seen to burn off the feet of those who walk through it for a long time. Men of this kind need no small time to cleanse the filth from their soul. There are others who have not been so unfortunate as to live out their lives in complete ignorance, and who have previously read the fourth of these commentaries. For them what has been said so far suffices by way of reminder, for the sake of hearing in an articulated way the names applied to each of their proper things, as all Greeks are accustomed to use them. I shall now pass to the second inquiry, which is no longer about a name and what is signified by it, but about the nature of a thing. Most people think that all things burnt become colder than they themselves were. Some, conversely, think that the heat of all burnt things is increased. Both err. For some will plainly appear to become hotter, in taste and touch and in the power observed in use, as I said earlier in the case of the pungent and the astringent things. Others, conversely, appear less hot after being burnt; and this we discern clearly both by sense and by use. By use I mean, as I said in the earlier cases, when, applied to the skin, some make it redder and hotter, others bloodless and cold, and some raise it into a swelling, while others contract it. The pungents, then, lose much of their heat when burnt, while those that are not such gain heat; but nothing burnt is completely cold. For there is left in them, as it were, a certain empyreuma (for Aristotle called it so), and this is what is washed away in washings. It is the most fine-particled part of the substance of burnt things. When it has gone off together with the water, the rest of the burnt thing is earthy substance. For burning consumes all the moisture, and what remains is earthy, together with what Aristotle called empyreuma. When, then, someone removes this and separates it by washing, the water in which the drug was washed has acquired a hot, fine-particled power, while the remainder becomes earthy and cold, able to dry without biting. I have spoken about these matters before as well, but it is no worse to recall them now too, so that someone who has the recollection of them fresh may follow more precisely what will be said.

    For acacia and balaustion are astringent, as are hypocistis and kytinoi and oak-gall and rhēon and sumach, omphakion and medlars, and cornels and pomegranate rinds and myrtle-berries.

  • B · drug names: named in the English only (ginger)
    δριμέα δ’ ἐστὶν εὐφόρβιον καὶ σκόροδα καὶ κρόμμυα καὶ πράσα καὶ νᾶπυ καὶ πέπερι καὶ γιγγίβερι καὶ σμύρνιον, ὀρίγανόν τε καὶ γλήχων, καὶ καλαμίνθη καὶ θύμος.
    Pungent are euphorbium and garlic and onions and leeks and mustard and pepper and ginger and smyrnion, oregano and pennyroyal, and calamint and thyme.
  • B · drug names: named in the English only (garlic)
    τὰ στύφοντα δ’ ἀποκρουόμενα τὸ περιεχόμενον ἐν αὐτοῖς τῷ ψύχειν τε καὶ συνάγειν καὶ πιλεῖν πέφυκεν.
    How, then, some say that pepper and garlic and all the pungents are astringent, it is not even possible to conceive.
  • B · drug names: named in the English only (pepper)

    How, then, some say that pepper and garlic and all the pungents are astringent, it is not even possible to conceive.
  • B · drug names: named in the English only (medlar)
    ἡ δύναμίς τε οὖν ἐναντιωτάτη τοῖς στύφουσίν ἐστι πρὸς τὰ δριμέα, καὶ ἡ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν ποιότης οὐδὲν ὅμοιον ἔχουσα.
    Suppose that, just as they call these astringent, they likewise called sumach and balaustion and omphakion and medlar and the other such things pungent, altering the usage of the Greeks in names.

    Suppose that, just as they call these astringent, they likewise called sumach and balaustion and omphakion and medlar and the other such things pungent, altering the usage of the Greeks in names.
  • B · drug names: named in the Greek only (pepper)
    πῶς οὖν ἔνιοι καὶ τὸ πέπερι καὶ τὰ σκόροδα καὶ πάντα τὰ δριμέα στύφειν λέγουσιν οὐδ’ ἐπινοῆσαι δυνατόν.
    In that case we would suppose them ignorant of the words of the Greeks together with what is signified by them, but not insensible in their gustatory or olfactory faculty.
